<!-- defi-1: body_md -->
\label{def:side_info_graph}
Given an index coding problem with $N$ users and datasets ${\bf X}_{[N]}$, the side information graph is a directed graph $G=(\mathcal{V}(G),\mathcal{E}(G))$, where $\mathcal{V}=[N]$ and $\mathcal{E}=\{ i\to j\}$, with $i\to j$ indicating that user $i$ knows dataset $X_j$.
<!-- end defi-1 -->
<!-- defi-2: body_md -->
\label{def:minrank}
Given a side information graph $G$, the minrank over $\mathbb{F}_q$ is~\cite{alon2020minrank}
\begin{align}
\label{eq:minrank}
\operatorname{minrk}_q(G)= \min\{\operatorname{{\rm rk}}({\bf A}) \ : \ {\bf A}=(a_{ij})\in\mathbb{F}_q^{N\times N}\,\,\mbox{fits}\,\, G \} 
\end{align}
where ${\bf A}$ fits $G$ if $a_{ii}\neq 0$ for all $i$, and $a_{ij}=0$ whenever edge $i \to j$ does not exist in $G$.
<!-- end defi-2 -->
<!-- theo-1: body_md -->
\label{theo:MAIS}
Given a side information graph $G=(\mathcal{V}(G),\mathcal{E}(G))$, let $G|_{\mathcal J}$ be the subgraph induced by $\mathcal J\subseteq \mathcal{V}(G)$. The size of the maximum acyclic induced subgraph is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:MAIS_bound}
{\mathrm{MAIS}}(G)=\max\limits_{\mathcal J\subseteq \mathcal{V}\ : \ G|_{\mathcal J}\ \mbox{is acyclic}}\ |\mathcal J| .
\end{align}
The length of any $\delta$-error randomized index code for $G$ is lower bounded by~\cite[Theorem~3]{bar2011index}
\begin{align}
R_{\mathrm{MAIS}}={\mathrm{MAIS}}(G)\cdot (1-h(\delta)) \ .
\end{align}
<!-- end theo-1 -->
<!-- defi-3: body_md -->
\label{def:modified_rook_graph}
$f_1f_2$-modified rook's graph $G^{f_1f_2}_{X_1X_2}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E})$ is a subgraph of the rook's graph on $\mathcal{X}_1\times\mathcal{X}_2$, defined as follows: $\mathcal{V}$ is the vertex set
 \begin{align}
 \label{eq:vertex_set_rook}
\mathcal{V}=\{((x_1^{(k)},x_2^{(k)})\in\mathcal{X}_1\times\mathcal{X}_2\} \ ,
 \end{align}
and $\mathcal{E}$ is the edge set
 \begin{align}
 \label{eq:edge_set_rook}
\mathcal{E}=\{\{(x_1^{(1)},x_2^{(1)}),(x_1^{(2)},x_2^{(2)})\}\ : \ &(x_1^{(1)},x_2^{(1)}),(x_1^{(2)},x_2^{(2)})\in\mathcal{V},\ (x_1^{(1)},x_2^{(1)})\neq (x_1^{(2)},x_2^{(2)})\ , \nonumber\\
&x_1^{(1)}=x_1^{(2)}\quad \mbox{and}\quad f_1(x_1^{(1)},x_2^{(1)})\neq f_1(x_1^{(2)},x_2^{(2)}) \ ,\nonumber\\
&x_2^{(1)}=x_2^{(2)}\quad \mbox{and}\quad f_2(x_1^{(1)},x_2^{(1)})\neq f_2(x_1^{(2)},x_2^{(2)})\} \ .
 \end{align}
<!-- end defi-3 -->
<!-- defi-4: body_md -->
\label{def:compatible_functions}
Functions $f_1(X_1, X_2)$ and $f_2(X_1, X_2)$ are compatible if there exists a function $f(X_1, X_2)$ such that $G^{ff}_{X_1X_2} = G^{f_1f_2}_{X_1X_2}$.
<!-- end defi-4 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
We denote random variables and their realizations in regular typeface, and matrices in boldface, with elements chosen from a finite field $\mathbb{F}_q$  of order $q\geq 2$, and the nonzero elements of $\mathbb{F}_q$ are denoted by $\mathbb{F}_q^*$. Let $\log$ and $\log_q$ be the binary logarithm and the logarithm in base $q > 2$, respectively. $\oplus_q$ denotes modulo-$q$ sum operation.
Given a pair of discrete random variables $(X,Y)$ with values over the space $\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal {Y}$, we denote by $P_{X,Y}$ their joint distribution, by $P_X$ and $P_Y$ their marginal distributions, respectively, and by $P_{X\mid Y}$ the conditional distribution of $Y$ given $X$. 
The Shannon entropies of $X$ in binary and q-ary units are given by $H_2(X)$ and $H_q(X) =\mathbb{E}[-\log_q P_X]= (1/ \log q)H_2(X)$, respectively, where $\mathbb{E}[X]$ is the expected value of a random variable $X$. Similarly, $H_q(X, Y)$ and $H_q(X \mid Y)$ denote the joint and conditional entropies, respectively. 
For a probability vector $\mathbf p=(p_1,\ldots,p_m)$, define $\mathsf H_q(\mathbf p)\triangleq-\sum_{j=1}^{m}p_j\log_q p_j$, where $0\log_q 0\triangleq 0$. 
The mutual information is defined as $I_q(X;Y)=D_{\mathrm {KL} }(P_{X,Y}\parallel P_X\otimes P_Y)$, where $D_{\mathrm {KL} }$ is the Kullback–Leibler divergence, and $P_X\otimes P_Y$ is the outer product distribution which assigns probability $P_X(x)\cdot P_Y(y)$ to each $(x,y)$. 
Given $X^n=X_1, X_2 \cdots, X_n$, an independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) sequence, each element $X_i$, $i\in [n]$ is distributed according to $P_X$.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- defi-5: body_md -->
\label{def-char-graph}
Let ${\bf X}_{[N]}=(X_1,\ldots,X_N)$ be a finite-alphabet source with distribution $P_{{\bf X}_{[N]}}$. Consider a receiver having side information $\mathcal S={\bf X}_{\mathcal A}$, $\mathcal A\subseteq[N]$, and requesting the function $f({\bf X}_{[N]})$.

The source characteristic graph associated with $({\bf X}_{[N]},\mathcal S,f)$ is $G_f=(\mathcal V, \mathcal E)$, where
\begin{align}
\label{eq:vertex_set}
\mathcal V \triangleq {\mathrm{supp}}\left(P_{{\bf X}_{[N]}}\right)\subseteq \prod_{j=1}^N\mathcal X_j \ , 
\end{align} 
and two distinct vertices ${\bf x}_{[N]},\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}\in\mathcal V$ are adjacent if and only if
\begin{align}
\label{eq:characteristic-graph-adjacency}
\left\{{\bf x}_{[N]},\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}\right\}\in\mathcal E\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,{\bf x}_{\mathcal A}=\widetilde{\bf x}_{\mathcal A}\ \text{and}\ f({\bf x}_{[N]})\neq f(\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}) .
\end{align}

\begin{comment}
              
                                         
                                                                                                                                                                                                           
                 
                                                      
                                                        
            
\end{comment}
<!-- end defi-5 -->
<!-- defi-6: body_md -->
\label{def-In-MIS independent set}
An independent set $\text{IS}$ in a graph $G(\mathcal{V}, \mathcal{E})$ is a subset of vertices with no edges between them. Such non-adjacent vertices can be encoded without ambiguity, i.e., assigned the same codeword. 
A maximal independent set (MIS) is an IS that is not a subset of any other independent set                                                                                   
~\cite{karp2009reducibility}. The set of all MISs of $G$ is denoted by $\Gamma(G)$, and an MIS of $G$ by $W\in \Gamma(G)$, respectively.
<!-- end defi-6 -->
<!-- defi-7: body_md -->
\label{def:Korner_graph_entropy}
Given a graph $G = (\mathcal{V} ,\mathcal{E})$ with a distribution on its vertices $\mathcal{V}$ and the set of all MISs $\Gamma(G)$, graph entropy is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:graph_entropy}
H_\kappa(G,{\bf X}_{[N]})                              
\triangleq\min_{\substack{P_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]}}:\\{\bf X}_{[N]}\in W,\;W\in\Gamma(G)}}I_q({\bf X}_{[N]};W)
=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n H_\chi\left(G^{\dot \vee n},{\bf X}_{[N]}^n\right) 
\end{align}
where ${\bf X}_{[N]}\in W \in \Gamma(G)$ means that the minimization is over all distributions $P_{{\bf X}_{[N]},W}({\bf x}_{[N]},w)$ such that $P_{{\bf X}_{[N]},W}({\bf x}_{[N]},w) > 0$ implies ${\bf x}_{[N]} \in w$, where $w$ is a MIS of $G$.
<!-- end defi-7 -->
<!-- defi-8: body_md -->
\label{def:or-power}
The $n$-fold OR power of a characteristic graph $G=(\mathcal V(G),\mathcal E(G))$ is denoted by
\begin{align}
G^{\dot \vee n}=\left(\mathcal V^n,\mathcal E^{\dot \vee n}\right) ,
\end{align}
which has vertex set $\mathcal V^n={\mathrm{supp}}\left(P_{\mathbf X_{[N]}^n}\right)=\left({\mathrm{supp}}\left(P_{\mathbf X_{[N]}}\right)\right)^n$, and two distinct sequences ${\bf x}_{[N]}^n,\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}^n\in\mathcal V^n$ are adjacent if they are adjacent in $G_f$ in at least one coordinate:
\begin{align}
\label{eq:or-power-adjacency}
\left\{{\bf x}_{[N]}^n,\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}^n\right\}\in\mathcal E^{\dot \vee n} \,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\, \exists t\in[n]:\left\{{\bf x}_{[N],t},\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N],t}\right\}\in\mathcal E \,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,
\exists t\in[n]:
\begin{cases}
{\bf x}_{\mathcal A,t}=\widetilde{\bf x}_{\mathcal A,t},\\
f({\bf x}_{[N],t})\neq f(\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N],t}).
\end{cases} 
\end{align}
<!-- end defi-8 -->
<!-- defi-9: body_md -->
\label{def:broadcast_graph}
For each $i\in[K]$, let $G_{f_i} =(\mathcal{V}, \mathcal{E}_i)$ be the characteristic graph associated with $(f_i,\mathcal S_i)$ according to~\eqref{eq:characteristic-graph-adjacency}, where $\mathcal{V}={\mathrm{supp}}\left(P_{{\bf X}_{[N]}}\right)$. The single-letter broadcast graph is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:single-letter_broadcast_graph}
G_{\mathrm B}\triangleq \bigcup_{i=1}^{K}G_{f_i}=\left(\mathcal V,\bigcup_{i=1}^{K}\mathcal E_i\right) . 
\end{align}
<!-- end defi-9 -->
<!-- defi-10: body_md -->
\label{def:blocklength_n_BC}
For each $i\in[K]$, let $G_{f_i}^{(n)}=\bigl(\mathcal X_{[N]}^n,\mathcal E_i^{(n)}\bigr)$ be the length-$n$ graph, where
\begin{align}
\label{eq:block_same_side_information_block_distinct_demands}                               
\mathcal E_i^{(n)}\triangleq\left\{\left\{{\bf x}_{[N]}^n,\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}^n\right\}:\;
{\bf x}_{\mathcal A_i}^n=\widetilde{\bf x}_{\mathcal A_i}^n,\ 
f_i^n({\bf x}_{[N]}^n)\neq f_i^n(\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}^n)
\right\} .
\end{align}
<!-- end defi-10 -->
<!-- defi-11: body_md -->
\label{def:operational-block-graph}
For each blocklength $n$, let                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  
\begin{align}
\label{eq:block_broadcast_graph}
G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}\triangleq\bigcup_{i=1}^K G_{f_i}^{(n)}=\left(\mathcal X_{[N]}^n,\mathcal E_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}\right) ,\qquad \text{where}\quad \mathcal E_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}\triangleq\bigcup_{i=1}^K\mathcal E_i^{(n)} ,
\end{align}
denote the operational block broadcast graph, where $\mathcal E_i^{(n)}$ is given in~\eqref{eq:block_same_side_information_block_distinct_demands}. 
Thus,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:block-broadcast-adjacency}
\bigl\{{\bf x}_{[N]}^n,\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}^n\bigr\}\in\mathcal E_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}
\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,
\exists i\in[K]:\bigl\{{\bf x}_{[N]}^n,\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}^n\bigr\}\in
\mathcal E_i^{(n)}
\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\, \exists i\in[K]:
\begin{cases}
{\bf x}_{\mathcal A_i}^n=\widetilde{\bf x}_{\mathcal A_i}^n,\\[1mm]
f_i^n({\bf x}_{[N]}^n)\neq f_i^n(\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}^n) .
\end{cases}
\end{align}
                                                                                        
Thus, two source sequences are adjacent in the length-$n$ broadcast graph if there exists one user whose side-information sequences are identical but whose demanded function sequences are different.
<!-- end defi-11 -->
<!-- defi-12: body_md -->
\label{def:zero-error-operational-entropy}
The chromatic entropy of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$ is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:operational-chromatic-entropy}
H_\chi\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^n\right)\triangleq\min\limits_{c_n\in\mathscr C(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})}H_q\left(c_n({\bf X}_{[N]}^n)\right) .
\end{align}
Defining $R_{n,0}^*(K,N)=\frac{1}{n}H_\chi\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^n\right)$, the asymptotic zero-error operational broadcast graph entropy is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:asymptotic-zero-error-broadcast-entropy}
R_0^*(K,N)                                             
\triangleq                               
\limsup_{n\to\infty}R_{n,0}^*(K,N)
                                 
\end{align}
where the existence of the limit is ensured by subadditivity\footnote{Concatenating proper colorings of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(m)}$ and $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$ produces a proper coloring of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(m+n)}$. Therefore, $H_\chi\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(m+n)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^{m+n}\right)\leq H_\chi\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(m)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^m\right)+H_\chi\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^n\right)$. Thus, Fekete's lemma gives $R_0^*(K,N)                                            
=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}H_\chi\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^n\right)$.} of the sequence $\{H_\chi\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^n\right)\}_{n\geq1}$.
<!-- end defi-12 -->
<!-- defi-13: body_md -->
\label{def:asymptotically-lossless-operational-entropy}
For $\epsilon\in(0,1)$, let
\begin{align}
H_{\chi,\epsilon}\Bigl(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\Bigr)\triangleq\min_{\substack{\mathcal T_n\subseteq\mathcal X_{[N]}^n:\\
\mathbb{P}({\bf X}_{[N]}^n\in\mathcal T_n)\geq1-\epsilon}}\;
\min_{c_n\in\mathscr C(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}[\mathcal T_n])} H_q\left(\widetilde c_n({\bf X}_{[N]}^n)\right) 
\label{eq:epsilon-chromatic-entropy}
\end{align}
denote the minimum chromatic entropy after excluding a set of source sequences having total probability at most $\epsilon$, 
where $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}[\mathcal T_n]$ is the subgraph induced by $\mathcal T_n\subseteq\mathcal X_{[N]}^n$, and $\widetilde c_n$ is the extension $\widetilde c_n({\bf x}_{[N]}^n)=c_n({\bf x}_{[N]}^n) \mathbf{1}\left\{{\bf x}_{[N]}^n\in\mathcal T_n\right\}+\mathsf e \mathbf{1}\left\{{\bf x}_{[N]}^n\notin\mathcal T_n\right\}$, with $\mathsf e$ denoting an error symbol, and $H_{\chi,\epsilon}$.

Defining $R_{n,\epsilon}^*(K,N)=\frac{1}{n}H_{\chi,\epsilon}\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\right)$, the asymptotically lossless operational broadcast graph entropy is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:asymptotically-lossless-operational-entropy}
R^*(K,N)                                                                
\triangleq\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}\limsup_{n\to\infty} R_{n,\epsilon}^*(K,N) .
\end{align}
<!-- end defi-13 -->
<!-- theo-2: body_md -->
\label{theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast}
Consider a $(K, N,\{\mathcal{S}_i\}_{i\in[K]},\{f_i\}_{i\in[K]})$ NFCB model, where $\mathcal S_i={\bf X}_{\mathcal A_i}$, $\mathcal A_i\subseteq[N]$. 
Let $G_{\mathrm B}\triangleq G_{\cup_{i=1}^{K}f_i}$ denote the broadcast characteristic graph, and let $\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$ denote the collection of independent sets of $G_{\mathrm B}$. 
Define 
\begin{align}
\label{eq-theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast}
 H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[N]}) \triangleq\min\limits_{\substack{P_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]}}:\\{\bf X}_{[N]}\in W,\;W\in\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})}} I_q({\bf X}_{[N]};W) .
\end{align}
Then $H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[N]})$ is achievable under the asymptotically lossless reconstruction criterion. Thus,
\begin{align}
R^*(K,N)\leq H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[N]}) 
\end{align}
where $H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[N]})$ is K\"orner's graph entropy of $\mathbf X_{[N]}$ with respect to the single-letter broadcast graph $G_{\mathrm B}$.
<!-- end theo-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
\label{proof_theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast} 
We denote a text channel attaining~\eqref{eq-theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast} by
\begin{align}
\label{eq:Wopt}
P^*_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]}}\in\arg\min_{\substack{P_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]}}:\\{\bf X}_{[N]}\in W,\;W\in\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})}}I_q({\bf X}_{[N]};W)
\end{align}
where $W$ denotes the independent-set-valued random variable\footnote{We may restrict $W$ to maximal independent sets, since every independent set can be enlarged to a maximal one.}                                                
induced by $P^*_{\mathbf X_{[N]},W}=P_{\mathbf X_{[N]}}P^*_{W\mid{\mathbf X}_{[N]}}$.

Assigning a unique codeword to each MIS in $\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$, which is determined by ${\bf X}_{[N]}$, $\{f_i\}_{i\in[K]}$, and $\{\mathcal{S}_i\}_{i\in[K]}$, ensures that each user $i\in[K]$ can correctly reconstruct $f_{i}$ from the received $M$ and $\mathcal{S}_i$.                                     
For asymptotically lossless recovery of all demands, the sender node builds the $n$-fold OR power graph\footnote{For more details on the $n$-fold OR products of characteristic graphs, we refer the reader to~\cite{AlonOrlit1996}.} $G^{\dot{\vee} n}_{\mathrm B}\triangleq G(\mathcal{V}^n ,\mathcal{E}^n)$ corresponding to the length-$n$ i.i.d realizations of $N$ datasets, namely ${\bf X}_{[N]}^{n}=\{(X_{1,t},\dots,X_{N,t})\}_{t\in [n]}$. 
The vertex set satisfies $\mathcal{V}^n=\{{\bf x}_{[N]}^n \triangleq \{{\bf x}_{[N],t}\}_{t\in [n]}                                                                                                      
\,:\, {\bf x}_{[N],t}\in \mathcal{V},\,\forall\, t\in [n]\}$ where each ${\bf x}_{[N],t}\triangleq (x_{1,t},x_{2,t},\dots,x_{N,t})$ is drawn i.i.d. from the same joint distribution $P_{X_1,X_2,\dots,X_N}$. 
The edge set satisfies $\mathcal{E}^n=\{({\bf x}_{[N]}^n,{{\bf x}'}_{[N]}^n)\, \mbox{if}\, \ \exists\, t\in[n] \, :\, ({\bf x}_{[N],t},{\bf x}'_{[N],t})\in \mathcal{E}\}$.                                                                                           


                         
\paragraph{Codebook generation}  
After building $G^{\dot{\vee} n}_{\mathrm B}$ using ${\bf X}_{[N]}^{n}$, $\{f_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in[K]}$, and $\{\mathcal{S}_{i}^{n}\}_{i\in[K]}$, the sender node employs random binning of the MISs $\{W^n\in\Gamma(G^{\dot{\vee} n}_{\mathrm B})\}$ via the mapping $en:\Gamma(G^{\dot{\vee} n}_{\mathrm B})\to [1, q^{nR(K,N)}]$. We refer to each subset of $W^n$ with the same index $m$ as a bin $\mathcal{B}(m)$, $m\in[1, q^{nR(K,N)}]$. The codebook, denoted as $\mathcal C=\{m\in [1,q^{nR}]\}$, is revealed to all users.



\paragraph{Encoding}
Upon observing $W^{n}$, the sender finds an index $m\in [1:q^{nR}]$ such that $W^n\in\mathcal{B}(m)$. Thus, the broadcast message is $en(W^n)=m$. The sender broadcasts the bin index $m$. 



\paragraph{Decoding}
Upon receiving $m$, the user declares $\hat{f}_{i}^{n}\triangleq F_i(W^n, \mathcal{S}_{i}^{n})$, a                    
function that denotes an estimate of               
$f_{i}^{n}$ if $W^n$ is the unique typical sequence in $\mathcal{B}(m)$. Otherwise, it declares an error.



\paragraph{Analysis of the probability of error}
We show that the probability of error averaged over bin assignments tends to zero as $n\to\infty$ if $R>H_q(W)$.

Let $M$ denote the random bin index of $W^n$, i.e., $W^n\in\mathcal{B}(M)$. Note that $M\sim{\mathrm Unif}[1,2^{nR}]$ is independent of $W^n$. User $i\in[K]$ makes an error if and only if at least one of the following occurs:
\begin{align}
                       
    E_{r_{i1}}=\{W^{n}\notin \mathcal{T}_{\epsilon}^{(n)}\} \ ,\quad
                         
    E_{r_{i2}}=\{\tilde{w}^{n}\in \mathcal{B}(M)\,\mbox{for some}\ \tilde{w}^{n}\neq W^n,\ \tilde{w}^n\in \mathcal{T}_{\epsilon}^{(n)}\} \nonumber
\end{align}
where $\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon}^{(n)}$ represents the set of $\epsilon$-typical $n$-sequences $W^n$ for some $\epsilon>0$. 
Then, by the symmetry of codebook construction, the union of events bound, random binning ensures that the probability of error averaged over bin assignments tends to zero as $n\to\infty$ if $R>H_q(W)+\delta(\epsilon)$ where $|\mathcal{T}_{\epsilon}^{(n)}|\leq q^{n(H_q(W)+\delta(\epsilon))}$. The details of the bounding of each probability of error term is standard and detailed in~\cite[Chapter~10.3.]{el2011network}.      


Thus, using random binning and generalizing~\eqref{eq:Rate_ach} to the broadcast setting, $\lim_{n\to\infty} P_{\mathrm{e}}^{(n)}=0$ holds when
\begin{align}
\label{eq-com-rate-main-mutual-info}
 R^*(K,N)\overset{(a)}{\leq}\min_{\substack{P_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]}}:\\{\bf X}_{[N]}\in W,\;W\in\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})}} I_q({\bf X}_{[N]} ;W) \overset{(b)}{=} H_\kappa\big(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[N]}\big) 
\end{align}
where in $(a)$, the minimization is over all $P_{\mathbf X_{[N]},W}>0$ such that $W\in\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$, and the inequality follows from noting that $W\in \Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$ is a function of ${\bf X}_{[N]}$ such that $H_q(f_i\mid W, \mathcal{S}_i)=0$ for all $i\in[K]$, ensuring $\lim_{n\to\infty} P_{\mathrm{e}}^{(n)}=0$, and $(b)$ follows from employing~\eqref{eq:graph_entropy}. The RHS in~\eqref{eq-com-rate-main-mutual-info} is achievable by assigning colors to                          
$G^{\dot{\vee} n}_{\mathrm B}$, which achieves the entropy bound of the coloring variable~\cite{korner1971coding,feizi2014network}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- cor-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:side-information-assisted-graph-bound}
Let the single-letter side-information-assisted quantity be                                   
\begin{align}
\label{eq:single_letter_side_info_assisted_def_G_B}
H_{\kappa,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}\left(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]}\mid\mathcal S_{[K]}\right)\triangleq\min_{\substack{P_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]}}:\\\substack{W-{\bf X}_{[N]}-(\mathcal S_1,\ldots,\mathcal S_K),\\{\bf X}_{[N]}\in W\in\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B}) \ \text{a.s.}}}}\max_{i\in[K]} I_q({\bf X}_{[N]};W\mid\mathcal S_i) .
\end{align}
Under the same assumptions as in Theorem~\ref{theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast}, a side-information-assisted independent-set coding scheme gives
\begin{align}
R^*(K,N)\leq H_{\kappa,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}\left(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]}\mid\mathcal S_{[K]}\right)\leq H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[N]})  .
\end{align}
<!-- end cor-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Fix a test channel $P_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]}}$ satisfying ${\bf X}_{[N]}\in W\in\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$ almost surely and $W-{\bf X}_{[N]}-(\mathcal S_1,\ldots,\mathcal S_K)$. 
Because every realization of $W$ is an independent set of $G_{\mathrm B}$, 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:function-from-W-Si}
H_q(f_i\mid W,\mathcal S_i)=0,
\qquad i\in[K] .
\end{align}
Indeed, if two source realizations in the same independent set had identical side information $\mathcal S_i$ but different values of $f_i$, they would be adjacent in $G_{f_i}$ and hence in $G_{\mathrm B}$.

Generate a random codebook containing $q^{n(I_q({\bf X}_{[N]};W)+\delta)}$ independently generated $W^n$ sequences. By the covering lemma, the encoder can find, with probability approaching one, a codeword jointly typical with ${\bf X}_{[N]}^n$. Randomly partition the codewords into $q^{nR}$ bins and transmit the bin index.

User~$i$ can recover the selected $W^n$ sequence from the received bin and its side-information sequence $\mathcal S_i^n$ whenever
\begin{align}
R>I_q({\bf X}_{[N]};W)-I_q(W;\mathcal S_i)\overset{(a)}{=}I_q({\bf X}_{[N]};W\mid\mathcal S_i) ,
\end{align}
where $(a)$ follows from $W-{\bf X}_{[N]}-\mathcal S_i$. Thus, the same bin index permits all users to recover $W^n$ provided that                                                                                      
\begin{align}
R>\max_{i\in[K]}\ I_q({\bf X}_{[N]};W\mid\mathcal S_i) .
\end{align}
Each user then reconstructs its demanded sequence symbolwise using~\eqref{eq:function-from-W-Si}. Since $K$ is finite, the union of all decoding-error events has probability tending to zero. 
Minimizing over the admissible test channels gives
\begin{equation}
R^*(K,N) \leq H_{\kappa,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}\left(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]}\mid\mathcal S_{[K]}\right) .\qedhere
\end{equation}
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{prop_achievable-rate-via-broadcast_compatible}
Given a $(K, N,\{\mathcal{S}_i\}_{i\in[K]},\{f_i\}_{i\in[K]})$ NFCB model, where $K=N$ and $\mathcal{S}_i=X_i$, for all $i\in[K]$, define the side-information-assisted chromatic-entropy bound as
\begin{align}
\label{eq:side-information-chromatic-bound}
H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]}\mid \mathcal S_{[K]}=\mathbf X_{[N]})\triangleq\min_{\substack{P_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]}}:\\{\bf X}_{[N]}\in W,\;W\overset{\circ}{\in}\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})}}\max_{i\in[K]} I_q({\bf X}_{[N]};W\mid X_i) .
\end{align}
Then, the broadcast rate satisfies
\begin{align}
\label{eq:prop_achievable-rate-via-broadcast_compatible}
 H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]})=R^*(K,N)=H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]}\mid \mathcal S_{[K]}=\mathbf X_{[N]})=\max\limits_{i\in[K]}\, H_q(f_i\mid X_i) .
\end{align}
provided that the compatibility condition in Definition~\ref{def:compatible_functions} holds.
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
The relation~\eqref{eq:side-information-chromatic-bound} implies  $H_q(W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]})=0$ because $W\overset{\circ}{\in}\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$, thus $W$ is a deterministic function of ${\bf X}_{[N]}$. It follows that
\begin{align}
\label{eq:side-information-chromatic-entropy}
H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]}\mid \mathcal S_{[K]}=\mathbf X_{[N]})=\min_{\substack{P_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[N]}}:\\{\bf X}_{[N]}\in W,\;W\overset{\circ}{\in}\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})}}\max_{i\in[K]}H_q(W\mid X_i) .
\end{align}

                            
Suppose that $\{f_i\}_{i\in[K]}$ are compatible, i.e., $\exists$ a common function $f:\mathcal X_{[N]}\longrightarrow\mathcal F$ such that, for every $i\in[K]$ and every pair ${\bf x}_{[N]},\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]}$ satisfying $x_i=\widetilde x_i$, we have $f({\bf x}_{[N]})=f(\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]})\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\, f_i({\bf x}_{[N]})=f_i(\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N]})$.
Equivalently,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:compatible-mutual-recovery}
H_q(f_i\mid f,X_i)=H_q(f\mid f_i,X_i)=0 ,
\qquad i\in[K] .
\end{align}

                                                                                                                                                                                                          

     
Indeed, for every feasible coloring variable $W$, we have $H_q(f_i\mid W,X_i)=0$, $i\in[K]$. Therefore,
\begin{align}
H_q(W\mid X_i)\geq I_q(f_i;W\mid X_i)=H_q(f_i\mid X_i) ,
\end{align}
and hence
\begin{align}
\label{eq:compatible-lower-direction}
H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]}\mid \mathcal S_{[K]}=\mathbf X_{[N]})
\geq
\max_{i\in[K]}H_q(f_i\mid X_i) .
\end{align}




Conversely, under~\eqref{eq:compatible-mutual-recovery}, the level sets
of $f$ are independent sets of $G_{\mathrm B}$. Thus, choosing the coloring variable as $W=f({\bf X}_{[N]})$ is feasible and gives
\begin{align}
\label{eq:compatible-upper-direction}
H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]}\mid \mathcal S_{[K]}=\mathbf X_{[N]})\leq\max_{i\in[K]}H_q(f\mid X_i)=\max_{i\in[K]}H_q(f_i\mid X_i),
\end{align}
where the last equality follows from~\eqref{eq:compatible-mutual-recovery}. Combining~\eqref{eq:compatible-lower-direction} and~\eqref{eq:compatible-upper-direction} proves~\eqref{eq:prop_achievable-rate-via-broadcast_compatible}.
 
\begin{comment}
                
             
                                                                                                    
           
                                                                                                                                                               
                                                                                                                                                                   
                                                  
           
                                                                                                                          
           
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             
             
                                                                                                                                    
           
\end{comment}
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
In Theorem~\ref{theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast}, the broadcast message is given by $M_n=en(W^n)$, where $W^n\in\Gamma(G^{\dot{\vee} n}_{\mathrm B})$. If, for a given user $i$ with $\mathcal{S}_i$ and demanded function $f_i$, two distinct source realizations induce the same function value, then these realizations are statistically indistinguishable from user $i$' s perspective. If, however, the demanded function of user $i'$ (where $i'\neq i$) differs across these realizations, then $f_{i'}$ cannot be a deterministic function of $\{M,\mathcal{S}_i, f_i\}$. Hence, for any source distribution that assigns positive probability to both realizations, $H_q\left(f_{i'}\mid M,\mathcal{S}_i,f_i\right)>0$, with a strict inequality. This establishes that while the encoding guarantees             
recovery of each user’s own demand, it does not, in general, eliminate uncertainty about the demands of other users. In general, the broadcast condition $\lim\limits_{n\to\infty} P_{\mathrm{e}}^{(n)}=0$ has a security  implication because $H_q(f_{i'}^n\mid M_n,\mathcal{S}_i^n,f_i^n) =H_q(g_{i',n}(M_n,\mathcal S_{i'}^n)\mid M_n,\mathcal \mathcal{S}_i^n,f_i^n)>0$, for some function $g_{i',n}$, unless $\mathcal{S}_{i'}$ is derived from $\{M_n,\mathcal{S}_i^n,f_i^n\}$.

\end{comment}
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- defi-14: body_md -->
\label{def:admissible-response-profiles}
For $\epsilon\in[0,1)$, let $\mathcal U_n(\epsilon)$ denote the
collection of random tuples $\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}=\bigl(U_1^{(n)},\ldots,U_K^{(n)}\bigr)$ satisfying the following conditions:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}$ is a deterministic function of
$\mathbf X_{[N]}^n$, i.e.,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:response-profile-source-function}
H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\bigr)=0 .
\end{align}

\item For every $i\in[K]$, there exists a reconstruction function $\eta_{i,n}:\mathcal U_i^{(n)}\times\mathsf S_i^n\longrightarrow\mathcal F_i^n$ such that
\begin{align}
\label{eq:response-profile-reconstruction}
\mathbb{P}\left(\eta_{i,n}\bigl(U_i^{(n)},\mathcal S_i^n\bigr)\neq f_i^n\right)\leq\epsilon .
\end{align}

\end{enumerate}
<!-- end defi-14 -->
<!-- theo-3: body_md -->
\label{theo-lower-bound-mutual-info}
For the $\bigl(K,N,\{\mathcal S_i\}_{i\in[K]},\{f_i\}_{i\in[K]}\bigr)$ NFCB problem, define
\begin{align}
\label{eq:multiletter-response-entropy-bound}
R_{\mathrm U}(K,N)&\triangleq\sup_{0<\epsilon<1}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\inf_{\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\in\mathcal U_n(\epsilon)}H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\bigr) ,\\
\label{eq:multiletter-conditional-bound}
R_{\mathrm c}(K,N)&\triangleq\sup_{0<\epsilon<1}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\inf_{\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\in\mathcal U_n(\epsilon)}\max_{i\in[K]}I_q\bigl(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n;\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr) .
\end{align}
Then,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:multiletter-converse-chain}
R^*(K,N)\geq R_{\mathrm U}(K,N)\geq R_{\mathrm c}(K,N)\geq\max_{i\in[K]}H_q(f_i\mid\mathcal S_i).
\end{align}
<!-- end theo-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Consider an arbitrary achievable sequence of deterministic
blocklength-$n$ codes. For each $n$, let $M_n=\psi_n(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n)$ be the common broadcast message, and let the reconstruction at user $i$ be
\begin{align}
\widehat f_i^n=g_{i,n}(M_n,\mathcal S_i^n),\qquad i\in[K] .
\end{align}
The block error probability $P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}\longrightarrow0$ (cf.~\eqref{eq:block-error-probability}) for an achievable sequence of codes.

For every message realization $m\in\mathcal M_n$, define the decoder-response function of user $i$ as
\begin{align}
\label{eq:decoder-response-function}
u_{i,m}^{(n)}:\mathsf S_i^n \longrightarrow \mathcal F_i^n, \qquad u_{i,m}^{(n)}(s_i^n) \triangleq g_{i,n}(m,s_i^n) .
\end{align}
The random response profile induced by the broadcast message is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:random-response-profile}
U_i^{(n)}\triangleq u_{i,M_n}^{(n)} .
\end{align}
Thus, the joint response profile induced by the broadcast message is $\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\triangleq \bigl(U_1^{(n)},\ldots,U_K^{(n)}\bigr)$. 
The response profile $U_i^{(n)}$ does not contain the realized side-information sequence $S_i^n$. Rather, it specifies the reconstruction produced by user $i$ for every possible side-information sequence. Once $M_n=m$ is fixed, the function $u_{i,m}^{(n)}$ is fixed. Therefore, $H_q\bigl(U_i^{(n)}\mid M_n\bigr)=0$, $i\in[K]$, and, jointly,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:joint-response-profile-given-message}
H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}
\mid M_n\bigr)=0.
\end{align}

Since $M_n=\psi_n(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n)$, the joint response profile is also a deterministic function of $\mathbf X_{[N]}^n$:
\begin{align}
\label{eq:joint-response-profile-given-source}
H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\bigr)=0.
\end{align}

By construction, $U_i^{(n)}(S_i^n)=g_{i,n}(M_n,\mathcal S_i^n)=\widehat f_i^n$. Therefore,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:response-profile-error}
\mathbb{P}\left(U_i^{(n)}(\mathcal S_i^n)\neq f_i^n\right)\leq P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}.
\end{align}
It follows from~\eqref{eq:joint-response-profile-given-source} and~\eqref{eq:response-profile-error} that 
                                     
$\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\in\mathcal U_n\bigl(P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}\bigr)$. 
 
Moreover, Fano's inequality gives  
                           
$H_q\bigl(f_i^n\mid U_i^{(n)},\mathcal S_i^n\bigr)\leq h_q\bigl(P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}\bigr)+P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}\log_q\bigl(|\mathcal F_i|^n-1\bigr)$ for every $i\in[K]$. 
Hence,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:asymptotic-response-profile-recovery}
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}H_q\bigl(f_i^n\mid U_i^{(n)},S_i^n\bigr)=0,\qquad i\in[K].
\end{align}

We first lower-bound the message entropy using the joint response
profile. From~\eqref{eq:joint-response-profile-given-message},
\begin{align}
\label{eq:message-response-profile-entropy}
H_q(M_n)\geq I_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)};M_n\bigr)
=H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\bigr)-H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid M_n\bigr)=H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}
\bigr) .
\end{align}
Therefore,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:finite-block-response-entropy-bound}
R_n(K,N)\geq\frac{1}{n}H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\bigr).
\end{align}

Fix any $\epsilon\in(0,1)$. Since
$P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}\to0$, for all sufficiently large $n$, $P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}\leq\epsilon$. Therefore, the response profile induced by the code belongs to $\mathcal U_n(\epsilon)$, and
\begin{align}
\label{eq:response-entropy-infimum}
R_n(K,N)\geq \frac{1}{n}\inf_{\mathbf V_{[K]}^{(n)}\in\mathcal U_n(\epsilon)}H_q\bigl(\mathbf V_{[K]}^{(n)}\bigr).
\end{align}
Using $\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}R_n(K,N)\leq R$ for every achievable rate $R$, we obtain
\begin{align}
R\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\inf_{\mathbf V_{[K]}^{(n)}\in\mathcal U_n(\epsilon)}H_q\bigl(\mathbf V_{[K]}^{(n)}\bigr) .
\end{align}
Taking the supremum over $\epsilon\in(0,1)$ and then the infimum over all achievable rates proves
\begin{align}
\label{eq:optimal-rate-response-entropy}
R^*(K,N)\geq R_{\mathrm U}(K,N).
\end{align}

Since $\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}$ is a deterministic function of $M_n$, for every $i\in[K]$,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:conditional-response-profile-bound}
H_q(M_n)\geq H_q(M_n\mid \mathcal S_i^n)\geq I_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)};M_n\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr)=H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr) .
\end{align}
Furthermore, because $\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}$ is a deterministic function of $\mathbf X_{[N]}^n$, it follows that 
              
                                                           
$H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr)=I_q\bigl(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n;\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr)$. 
Thus, we derive the conditional mutual-information bound:
\begin{align}
\label{eq:finite-block-conditional-response-bound}
R_n(K,N)\geq\frac{1}{n}\max_{i\in[K]}I_q\bigl(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n;\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr).
\end{align}
Applying the same infimum and asymptotic argument used above
gives
\begin{align}
\label{eq:optimal-rate-conditional-response}
R^*(K,N)\geq R_{\mathrm c}(K,N) .
\end{align}

For every admissible $\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}$ and every $i\in[K]$,
\begin{align}
H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}
\bigr)\geq H_q\bigl(\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr)=I_q\bigl(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n;\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr) .
\end{align}
Therefore,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:response-bound-ordering}
R_{\mathrm U}(K,N)\geq R_{\mathrm c}(K,N) .
\end{align}

Finally, for any $i\in[K]$,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:single-user-functional-lower-bound}
I_q\bigl(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n;\mathbf U_{[K]}^{(n)}\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr)\geq I_q\bigl(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n; U_i^{(n)}\mid\mathcal S_i^n\bigr)&\geq I_q\bigl(f_i^n;U_i^{(n)}\mid \mathcal S_i^n\bigr)\nonumber\\
&=H_q(f_i^n\mid\mathcal S_i^n)-H_q\bigl(f_i^n\mid U_i^{(n)},\mathcal S_i^n\bigr) ,
\end{align}
where the second inequality follows from data processing because $f_i^n$ is a deterministic function of $\mathbf X_{[N]}^n$. Using the temporal i.i.d. assumption, $H_q(f_i^n\mid S_i^n)=nH_q(f_i\mid S_i)$, and using
\eqref{eq:asymptotic-response-profile-recovery}, we obtain
\begin{align}
\label{eq:hardest-user-lower-bound}
R_{\mathrm c}(K,N)\geq\max_{i\in[K]}H_q(f_i\mid S_i).
\end{align}
Combining~\eqref{eq:optimal-rate-response-entropy},~\eqref{eq:response-bound-ordering}, and~\eqref{eq:hardest-user-lower-bound} proves~\eqref{eq:multiletter-converse-chain}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- defi-15: body_md -->
\label{def:clique_graph_theory}
A clique in a graph $G=(\mathcal{V}(G),\mathcal{E}(G))$ is a collection of vertices, where every two are connected. We denote by $\Omega(G)$ the collection of cliques of $G$.
<!-- end defi-15 -->
<!-- defi-16: body_md -->
\label{def:clique}
For a random variable $X$ distributed over $\mathcal V$ of $G$, the clique entropy is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:clique_entropy}
H_\omega(G,X)\triangleq\max_{\substack{P_{W'|X}:\\
X\in W'\in\Omega(G)}}H_q(X\mid W'),
\end{align}
which is given in base-$q$, where $W'$ is a random clique of $G$ containing the realized vertex $X$.
<!-- end defi-16 -->
<!-- theo-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:zero_error_clique_converse}
For any zero-error computation-broadcast code,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:zero_error_clique_bound}
H_q(M_n)\geq H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}, \mathbf X_{[N]}^n\right).
\end{align}
Thus, the optimal zero-error broadcast rate satisfies
\begin{align}
\label{eq:asymptotic_zero_error_clique_bound}
R_0^*(K,N)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\right) .
\end{align}
<!-- end theo-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Consider an arbitrary blocklength-$n$ zero-error code with encoder
\begin{align}
M_n=\psi_n(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n).
\end{align}
We first show that $\psi_n$ is a proper coloring of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$.

Consider two adjacent vertices $\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n$ and $\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{[N]}^n$ of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$. By Definition~\ref{eq:block_broadcast_graph}, there exists $i\in[K]$ such that
\begin{align}
s_i^n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n)=s_i^n(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{[N]}^n)\quad\text{and}\quad f_i^n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n)\neq f_i^n(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{[N]}^n) .
\end{align}
Suppose, by contradiction, that
\begin{align}
\psi_n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n)=\psi_n(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{[N]}^n) .
\end{align}
User $i$ would then receive the same message and possess the same side-information sequence under both source realizations. Its decoder would thus produce the same reconstruction in the two cases, contradicting the zero-error requirement. Hence, $\psi_n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n)\neq\psi_n(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{[N]}^n)$, and $\psi_n$ is a proper coloring of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$.

Now let $W'_n$ be an arbitrary random clique satisfying
\begin{align}
\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\in W'_n\in\Omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}) .
\end{align}
Because $\psi_n$ is a proper coloring and every pair of distinct vertices in $W'_n$ is adjacent, $\psi_n$ is one-to-one when restricted to $W'_n$. Hence, given $(M_n,W'_n)$, the source sequence $\mathbf X_{[N]}^n$ is uniquely determined:
\begin{align}
H_q(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\mid M_n,W'_n)=0.
\end{align}
It follows that
\begin{align}
\label{eq:clique_entropy_intermediate}
H_q(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\mid W'_n)=I_q(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n;M_n\mid W'_n)\leq H_q(M_n\mid W'_n)\leq H_q(M_n) .
\end{align}
Maximizing the left-hand side of~\eqref{eq:clique_entropy_intermediate} over all                            
$P_{W'_n|\mathbf X_{[N]}^n}$ satisfying $\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\in W'_n\in\Omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})$ gives
\begin{align}
H_q(M_n)\geq H_\omega \left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n \right) .
\end{align}
Dividing by $n$ and taking the asymptotic limit yields~\eqref{eq:asymptotic_zero_error_clique_bound}.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
For zero-error codes, the preimage of every message $M_n$ is an independent set of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$. Hence, $M_n$ is a proper coloring, and
\begin{align}
\label{eq:ordering_of_various_graph_quantities}
H_q(M_n)\geq H_\chi\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^n\right)\geq H_\kappa\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\right)\geq H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^n\right) 
\end{align}
which applies to a deterministic zero-error code (cf.~\eqref{eq:asymptotic-zero-error-broadcast-entropy}).                                                                                                                                                                                   
K\"orner’s graph entropy in~\eqref{eq:ordering_of_various_graph_quantities} is an asymptotic quantity given by $H_\kappa\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[N]}^n\right)= \lim\limits_{m\to\infty} \frac1m H_\chi\left((G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})^{\dot\vee m},\mathbf (X_{[N]}^n)^m\right)$ (cf.~\eqref{eq:graph_entropy}), where $(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})^{\dot \vee m}\neq G_{\mathrm B}^{(nm)}$ in general. The OR power allows the user witnessing confusability to vary across the $m$ blocks, whereas $G_{\mathrm B}^{(nm)}$ requires one fixed user to be confused over the complete length-$nm$ block. 
 
However, in the asymptotically lossless setting, detailed below, the corresponding graph quantities must be evaluated on the high-probability correct-decoding subset.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theo-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:vanishing_error_clique_converse}
The optimal asymptotically lossless computation-broadcast rate satisfies
\begin{align}
\label{eq:vanishing_error_clique_bound}
R^*(K,N)\geq\sup_{0<\epsilon<1}(1-\epsilon)
\limsup_{n\to\infty} \frac{1}{n}L_n(\epsilon) .
\end{align}
In particular, whenever the corresponding limit as $\epsilon\downarrow0$ exists,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:vanishing_error_clique_bound_limit}
R^*(K,N)\geq\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}(1-\epsilon)\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}L_n(\epsilon) .
\end{align}
<!-- end theo-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Consider an arbitrary blocklength-$n$ code with encoder $\psi_n$ and decoders $\{g_{i,n}\}_{i\in[K]}$. Define its high-probability source set on which all users decode correctly as
\begin{align}
\label{eq:good_source_set}
\mathcal T_n \triangleq \bigl\{\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n:\ g_{i,n}\bigl(\psi_n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n),s_i^n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n)\bigr)=f_i^n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n),\quad\forall i\in[K]\bigr\}.
\end{align}
By the definition of the block error probability, $P_{\mathbf X_{[N]}^n}(\mathcal T_n)=1-P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}$.

We claim that the restriction of $\psi_n$ to $\mathcal T_n$ is a proper coloring of the induced graph $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}[\mathcal T_n]$. To see this, consider adjacent vertices $\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n,\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{[N]}^n\in\mathcal T_n$. There exists a user $i\in[K]$ such that
\begin{align}
s_i^n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n)=s_i^n(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{[N]}^n)\quad\text{and}\quad f_i^n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n)\neq f_i^n(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{[N]}^n).
\end{align}
If both source sequences produced the same message, user $i$ would receive identical decoder inputs in both cases and hence produce identical reconstructions. The decoder could not then be correct for both source sequences, contradicting their membership in $\mathcal T_n$. Therefore,
\begin{align}
\psi_n(\mathbf{x}_{[N]}^n)\neq\psi_n(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{[N]}^n).
\end{align}

Let $E_n\triangleq\mathbbm{1}\{\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\in\mathcal T_n\}$ and let $p_n\triangleq\mathbb{P}(E_n=1)=1-P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}$. Conditioned on $E_n=1$, the encoder is a proper coloring of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}[\mathcal T_n]$. Applying Theorem~\ref{thm:zero_error_clique_converse} to the induced graph and the conditional source distribution gives
\begin{align}
\label{eq:conditional_good_set_clique_bound}
H_q(M_n\mid E_n=1)\geq H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}[\mathcal T_n],\mathbf X_{[N]}^n
\,\middle|\,\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\in\mathcal T_n\right) .
\end{align}

Moreover,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:message_entropy_good_set}
H_q(M_n)\geq H_q(M_n\mid E_n)=p_n H_q(M_n\mid E_n=1)+(1-p_n)H_q(M_n\mid E_n=0)\geq p_n H_q(M_n\mid E_n=1).
\end{align}
Combining~\eqref{eq:conditional_good_set_clique_bound} and~\eqref{eq:message_entropy_good_set} yields
\begin{align}
\label{eq:finite_block_clique_converse}
H_q(M_n)\geq (1-P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)})H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}[\mathcal T_n],\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\,\middle|\, \mathbf X_{[N]}^n\in\mathcal T_n\right)\geq (1-P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)})L_n(P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}) .
\end{align}

Fix any $\epsilon\in(0,1)$. For any achievable sequence of codes, $P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}\to0$, and hence $P_{\mathrm e}^{(n)}\leq\epsilon$ for all sufficiently large $n$. The corresponding set $\mathcal T_n$ then satisfies $P_{\mathbf X_{[N]}^n}(\mathcal T_n)\geq1-\epsilon$. 
Thus, for all sufficiently large $n$,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:fixed_epsilon_clique_bound}
R_n(K,N)=\frac1nH_q(M_n)\geq\frac{1-\epsilon}{n}L_n(\epsilon) .
\end{align}
Taking $\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}$ and using $\limsup\limits_{n\to\infty}R_n(K,N)\leq R$ for every achievable rate $R$, we obtain for every $\epsilon\in(0,1)$
\begin{align}
R\geq(1-\epsilon)\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1nL_n(\epsilon) .
\end{align}
Taking the supremum over $\epsilon$ and then the infimum over all achievable rates proves~\eqref{eq:vanishing_error_clique_bound}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lem-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:smoothing_lemma}
For each blocklength $n\in\mathbb N$, and $\epsilon\in(0,1/2)$,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:smoothing_bound}
H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\right)-h_q(\epsilon)-\epsilon n\log_q|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B})|\leq L_n(\epsilon)\leq H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\right) .
\end{align}
Thus,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:regularized-smoothing-equality}
\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow0}(1-\epsilon)\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}L_n(\epsilon)=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\right) .
\end{align}
<!-- end lem-1 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The result follows from a standard entropy-continuity argument. For each blocklength $n\in\mathbb N$, define $b_n\triangleq H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\right)$. Let $Z_n$ be a clique-valued auxiliary random variable achieving $b_n$, and let $E_n=\mathbbm 1\{\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\in\mathcal T_n\}$. Conditioned on $E_n=1$, the random set $Z_n\cap\mathcal T_n$ remains a clique of the induced graph $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}[\mathcal T_n]$ and contains $\mathbf X_{[N]}^n$~\cite{alon1996source,CovTho1991}. Decomposing $H_q(\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\mid Z_n)$ according to $E_n$ and bounding the contribution of the event $E_n=0$ by $h_q(\epsilon)+\epsilon n\log_q|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B})|$ gives
\begin{align}
b_n-h_q(\epsilon)-\epsilon n\log_q|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B})|\leq L_n(\epsilon)\leq b_n.
\end{align}
The upper bound follows by choosing $\mathcal T_n=\mathcal V^n(G_{\mathrm B})$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{prop-genie}   
Let $(K,N,\{\mathcal{S}_i\}_{i\in[K]},\{f_i\}_{i\in[K]})$ be a NFCB model. For a given                   
$\pi \in \mathrm{Perm}([K])$, we assume that a genie reveals to each user $\pi(i)$, for $i\geq 2$, the following:
\begin{itemize}
    \item the side information sets $\{\mathcal{S}_{\pi(i')}\}_{i' \in [i-1]}$, and  
    \item the demanded functions $\{f_{\pi(i')}\}_{ i' \in [i-1]}$ decoded by users $\pi(1), \dots, \pi(i-1)$.  
\end{itemize} 

Then, the broadcast rate is lower-bounded as
\begin{align}
\label{eq-genie-aided-rate}
R^*(K,N) \geq \max_{\pi \in \mathrm{Perm}([K])} \left [H_q(f_{\pi(1)}\mid \mathcal{S}_{\pi(1)})+\sum_{i=2}^{K} H_q\big( f_{\pi(i)} \mid \{f_{\pi(i')},\mathcal{S}_{\pi(i')}\}_{i' \in[i-1]},\ \mathcal{S}_{\pi(i)} \big)\right] \ .
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Given a user permutation $\pi$, the broadcast rate lower bound in~\eqref{eq-genie-aided-rate} is achieved using a genie-aided scheme, where each user $\pi(i)\in[K]$ decodes $f_{\pi(i)}$ using their own side information $\mathcal{S}_{\pi(i)}$ and the data $\bigcup_{i' \leq i} \mathcal{S}_{\pi(i')}$ of all preceding users, according to the permutation $\pi$. 

With this genie-aided scheme, for a given $i\in[K]$, user $\pi(1)$ must receive at least $H_q( f_{\pi(1)} \mid \mathcal{S}_{\pi(1)})$ bits, and user $\pi(i)$, with $i\geq 2$, at least  $H_q\big( f_{\pi(i)} \mid \{f_{\pi(i')},\mathcal{S}_{\pi(i')}\}_{i' \in[i-1]},\ \mathcal{S}_{\pi(i)} \big)$ bits to recover their requested functions, respectively. The total broadcast rate of the sender given $\pi$ is lower bounded as
 \begin{align}
 \label{eq-genie-aided-rate-1}
     H_q(f_{\pi(1)}\mid \mathcal{S}_{\pi(1)})+\sum_{i=2}^{K} H_q\big( f_{\pi(i)} \mid \{f_{\pi(i')},\mathcal{S}_{\pi(i')}\}_{i' \in[i-1]},\ \mathcal{S}_{\pi(i)} \big) \ \mbox{ bits.}
 \end{align}
The final result follows from considering all possible permutations $\mathrm{Perm}([K])$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{prop-2-user-rate_optimality}
Given a $(2,2,\{X_1,X_2\},\{f_1,f_2\})$ NFCB model, the rate
\begin{align}
\label{eq-2-user-gap}
H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[2]})=R^*(2,2)=\max\ (H_q(f_1\mid X_1),H_q(f_2\mid X_2))  \,\mbox{bits} 
\end{align} 
is optimum when $f_1$, $f_2$ are compatible functions or when $q=2$.
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
\label{proof_prop-2-user-rate_optimality}

For $N=K=2$, and  $\mathcal{S}_i=X_i$, $i\in\{1,2\}$, from Theorem~\ref{theo-lower-bound-mutual-info} and Proposition~\ref{prop-genie},                                           
it holds 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:converse_K2}
R^*(2,2)\geq \max\ (H_q(f_1\mid X_1),H_q(f_2\mid X_2)) \ . 
\end{align}

We next consider the following two scenarios:

(i) When $f_1$, $f_2$ are compatible functions, following Definition~\ref{def:compatible_functions} (cf.~\cite{ravi2016broadcast}), there exists a deterministic function $f(X_1, X_2)$ such that $G^{ff}_{X_1X_2}=G^{f_1f_2}_{X_1X_2}$, implying that $H_q(f_1\mid X_1,f)=H_q(f_2\mid X_2,f)=0$. Hence, $H_q(f\mid X_i)=H_q(f_i \mid X_i)$ for $i\in[2]$. Using similar arguments, $H_q(f\mid X_1,f_1)=H_q(f\mid X_2,f_2)=0$. 
From Proposition~\ref{prop_achievable-rate-via-broadcast_compatible}, the following rate is achievable and optimal due to~\eqref{eq:converse_K2}:
\begin{align}
\label{eq:prop_achievable-rate-via-broadcast_compatible_K2}
H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[2]})=\max \ (H_q(f_1\mid X_1),H_q(f_2\mid X_2))
\end{align}

\begin{comment}
                                                      
             
                                                                                                                                                                                 
                                                                                                                 
                   
           
                                                                                                                  
                                                                                                                                                                          
\end{comment}


(ii) When $q=2$, any $G_{X_1X_2}^{f_1f_2}$ is a subgraph of a graph with an edge set determined by $f_1(0,0)\neq f_1(0,1)$, $f_1(1,0)\neq f_1(1,1)$, $f_2(0,0)\neq f_2(1,0)$, and $f_2(0,1)\neq f_2(1,1)$, employing~\eqref{eq:vertex_set_rook} and~\eqref{eq:edge_set_rook} for $\mathcal{X}_1=\mathcal{X}_2=\mathbb{F}_2$, meaning that $G_{X_1X_2}^{f_1f_2}$ has at most $4$ edges. 
Similarly, any $G_{X_1X_2}^{ff}$ is a subgraph of a graph with an edge set determined by $f(0,0)\neq f(0,1)$, $f(1,0)\neq f(1,1)$, $f(0,0)\neq f(1,0)$, and $f(0,1)\neq f(1,1)$, meaning that $f(0,1)=f(1,0)$ and $f(0,0)=f(1,1)$. 
 
If there is no $f$ such that  $G_{X_1X_2}^{ff}= G_{X_1X_2}^{f_1f_2}$, then following the steps of proof of~\cite[Theorem~3]{ravi2016broadcast}, the only scenario where $f_1$, $f_2$ are not compatible is if graph $G^{f_1f_2}_{X_1X_2}$ has one edge. Any other possible        
$G^{f_1f_2}_{X_1X_2}$ is compatible. For those compatible graphs, the proof follows from part (i). For a graph with only one edge resulting from $f_1(0,0)\neq f_1(0,1)$, it is clear that $H_2(f_2 \mid X_2) = 0$, i.e., user $2$ can recover $f_2$ only from $X_2$. 
User $1$ requires an encoding rate $H_2(f_1\mid X_1)$. Thus, the rate $\max\ (H_2(f_1\mid X_1), H_2(f_2\mid X_2 )) = H_2(f_1\mid X_1)$ is achievable.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- prop-4: body_md -->
\label{prop-3-user-gap-genie}
Given a $(3,3,\{X_i\}_{i\in[3]},\{f_i\}_{i\in[3]})$ NFCB model for general $\{f_1,f_2,f_3\}$ with $q=2$, the worst-case gap over all possible topologies is
\begin{align}
\label{eq-theo-3-user-gap}
\max\limits_{\mathcal{N}\in \mathfrak{N}}\,\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(3,3)= 0.5  \,\mbox{bits} 
\end{align} 
where $\mathcal{N}$ and $\mathfrak{N}$ denote a specific topology and the class of all possible topologies, respectively.
The average gap over all possible topologies in Tables~\ref{tab:my_label-2}-                             
\ref{table-gap-3-diff}, with each topology being equally likely, is
\begin{align}
\Delta(3,3)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{N}}[\Delta_{\mathfrak{N}}(3,3)]=0.125 \,\mbox{bits}\ .
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
\label{proof-theo-3-user-gap-genie}
Setting $\mathcal{S}_i=X_i$ for all $i\in[K]$ and using $q=2$, we note that
 
\begin{comment}
                         
                                                                                                                                                                           
             
                                  
                                                                                                                                                                                  
                                               
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         
           
                                                                                                  
\end{comment}
 
\begin{align}
\label{eq-proof-lem-3-gap-1}
I_2({\bf X}_{[N]};W\mid X_i)=H_2(W\mid X_i)=H_2(f_i\mid X_i)+H_2(W \mid f_i, X_i) 
\end{align}
where $W$ is the MIS induced by $P^*_{\mathbf X_{[N]},W}$, given in~\eqref{eq:Wopt}. 
Without loss of generality, let us assume that the maximum $H_{\kappa,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}\left(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[N]}\mid\mathcal S_{[K]}\right)$ (cf.~\eqref{eq:single_letter_side_info_assisted_def_G_B}) in~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-gap-1} is attained by user~$i=1$ with demand $f_1$ given $\mathcal{S}_1=X_1$. Thus,
\begin{align}
\label{eq-proof-lem-3-gap-2}
    H_{\kappa,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}\left(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid\mathcal S_{[3]}\right)=H_2(f_1\mid X_1)+H_2(W\mid f_1, X_1) \ . 
\end{align} 

From~\eqref{eq-genie-aided-rate}, we calculate the genie-aided lower bound as follows:
\begin{align}
\label{eq-proof-lem-3-user-gap-genie-2}  
R^*(3,3)
&\geq \max_{\substack{(i_1,i_2,i_3)\in \mathrm{Perm}([3])}}\ \sum\limits_{l=1}^3 \ H_2(f_{i_l}\mid X_{i_1},\cdots,X_{i_l},f_{i_1},\cdots f_{i_{l-1}})
                                                                                                                                                
                                                                                                                                                                                            
\end{align}
where $H_2(f_{i_3}\mid X_{i_1},X_{i_2},X_{i_3},f_{i_1},f_{i_2})=0$ for any $(i_1,i_2,i_3)\in \mathrm{Perm}([3])$.

 the union graph $G_{\mathrm B} = G(\mathcal{V}, \mathcal{E})$ and assuming that the maximum in~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-gap-1} is attained by user~$i=1$, we next define three classes of edges, namely $\mathcal{E}_1$, $\mathcal{E}_2$, and $\mathcal{E}_3$, where 
\begin{align}
\mathcal{E}_1 &= \{({\bf x}_{[3]},{\bf x}_{[3]}') \in \mathcal{E} : ({\bf x}_{[3]},{\bf x}_{[3]}') \,\mbox{induced solely by } f_1\mid {X_1}\} , \\
\mathcal{E}_2 &= \{({\bf x}_{[3]},{\bf x}_{[3]}') \in \mathcal{E} : ({\bf x}_{[3]},{\bf x}_{[3]}') \: \mbox{induced by } f_2\mid {X_2} \mbox{ and } f_1\mid {X_1}\} ,\\
\mathcal{E}_3 &= \{({\bf x}_{[3]},{\bf x}_{[3]}') \in \mathcal{E} : ({\bf x}_{[3]},{\bf x}_{[3]}') \: \mbox{induced by } f_3\mid{X_3} \mbox{ and } f_1\mid{X_1} \}  
\end{align}
such that $\mathcal{E}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{3}\mathcal E_i$\footnote{Note that we do not need to consider the set of edges induced by all $f_1\mid X_1$, $f_2\mid X_2$, and $f_3\mid X_3$, as this set is empty.}, where ${\bf x}_{[3]}=(x_1,x_2,x_3)$ and ${\bf x}_{[3]}'=(x_1',x_2',x_3')$. 
More specifically,  $\mathcal{E}_1$ includes edges induced solely by $f_1\mid X_1$, and $\mathcal{E}_2$ by both $f_1\mid X_1$ and $f_2\mid X_2$, and $\mathcal{E}_3$ by both $f_1\mid X_1$ and $f_3\mid X_3$.  
The additive gap, denoted by $\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(3,3)$, between~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-gap-2} and~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-user-gap-genie-2} for $3$-user NFCB is  
\begin{align}
\label{eq-proof-lem-3-user-gap-genie-3}
\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(3,3)
&\overset{(a)}{\leq} H_2(f_1\mid X_1)+H_2(W\mid f_1, X_1)-\{H_2(f_1\mid X_1)+\max\,\{H_2(f_2\mid X_1,X_2,f_1), \: H_2(f_3\mid X_1,X_3,f_1)\}\}\nonumber\\
&\overset{}{=}H_2(W\mid f_1, X_1)-\max_{\substack{(i_2,i_3)\in \mathrm{Perm}([3]\setminus\{1\})}}\{H_2(f_{i_2}\mid X_1, X_{i_2},f_1) \} 
\end{align}
where $(a)$ follows from employing $i_1=1$ in~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-user-gap-genie-2}, and from~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-gap-2}. We illustrate this gap for different $\{f_i\}_{i\in [K]}$ in Tables~\ref{tab:my_label-2},~\ref{tab:my_label-3}, and~\ref{table-gap-3-diff}. For each distinct scenario, $\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(3,3)$ is calculated by subtracting the first column, representing $G_{W \mid f_1, X_1}$, from the maximum of the second and third columns, which correspond to $G_{f_2 \mid X_1, X_2, f_1}$ and $G_{f_3 \mid X_1, X_3, f_1}$, respectively.                                                                                                                      
We indicate distinct edge classes using distinct line styles for different source tuples ${\bf x}_{[3]}=(x_1,x_2,x_3)\in\mathbb{F}_2^3$. More specifically, $\mathcal{E}_1$ is absent (due to conditioning on $X_1$ and $f_1$), $\mathcal{E}_2$, and $\mathcal{E}_3$ are denoted by long-dashed lines and short-dashed lines, respectively. 


In $G_{W \mid f_1, X_1}$, since we condition on $X_1$, there exist no cross edges between sets $\{(000),(001), (010),(011)\}$ and $\{(100),(101),(110),(111)\}$.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       
For $X_1=0$, corresponding to the right half of $G_{W \mid f_1, X_1}$, the edges $\{(000),(011)\}$ and $\{(001),(010)\}$ do not appear in the graph, as they are induced solely by $f_1$, and the graph is constructed conditioned on knowing $f_1$. Similarly, for $X_1=1$, corresponding to the left half of $G_{W \mid f_1, X_1}$, edges $\{(100),(111)\}$ and $\{(101),(110)\}$ are also absent for the same reason. Thus, no complete subgraph is induced by the functions on either side of $G_{W\mid f_1, X_1}$.







To characterize $\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(3,3)$ in~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-user-gap-genie-3}, consider a fixed value of $X_1$ and an arbitrary $f_1$. Without loss of generality, assume that the maximum of the two terms on the RHS of~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-user-gap-genie-3} is attained by $H_2(f_2\mid f_1, X_1, X_2)$. We then partition the dataset tuples $(X_1, X_2, X_3)$ into two subgraphs based on whether $X_1=0$ or $X_1=1$. 
In $G_{W \mid f_1, X_1}$, where each subgraph has $4$ vertices and at most $6$ edges ($K_4$ is the complete graph on four vertices). 
                                                                                                                                  
A subset of these edges, labeled $\mathcal{E}_1$, are removed due to knowledge of $(f_1, X_1)$, while the remaining edges, labeled $\mathcal{E}_2$ (long-dashed lines) and $\mathcal{E}_3$ (short-dashed lines), remain.


If $G_{f_2 \mid f_1, X_1, X_2}$ retains all edges in $\mathcal{E}_2$, shown in Tables~\ref{tab:my_label-2} (Top Row), then $H_2(W\mid f_1, X_1)=H_2(f_2 \mid f_1, X_1, X_2)\leq 1$, and thus $\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(3,3) = 0$. Conversely, the maximum value of $\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(3,3)$ is attained when $G_{f_2\mid X_1,X_2,f_1}$ contains only a subset of $\mathcal{E}_2$, e.g., either the subsets $\{(110),(111)\}$, $\{(101),(100)\}$, shown in Tables~\ref{tab:my_label-2} (Bottom Row), or from symmetry $\{(000),(001)\}$, $\{(011),(010)\}$, resulting in $\max\limits_{\mathcal{N}\in \mathfrak{N}}\,\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(3,3)=0.5$ bits, hence $\Delta(3,3)\leq 0.5$ bits. 

The average gap over all possible topologies summarized in Tables~\ref{tab:my_label-2}, \ref{tab:my_label-3}, \ref{table-gap-3-diff} --- assuming that each topology is equally likely --- can be observed to be $\Delta(3,3)=0.125$ bits.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- prop-5: body_md -->
\label{prop-4-user-gap-genie}
Given a general $(4,4,\{X_i\}_{i\in[4]},\{f_i\}_{i\in[4]})$ NFCB model with $q=2$, the worst-case gap over all possible topologies (see Tables~\ref{tab:Subgraphs derived from Gf-4user}-\ref{tab:entropyvalues-4user} and Figures~\ref{fig:Gf1f2_Gf1f3_Gf1f4}-                                          
\ref{fig:Gf1f3f4_Gf1f4f2_Gf1f4f3}) is
\begin{align}
\label{eq-theo-4-user-gap}
\max\limits_{\mathcal{N}\in \mathfrak{N}}\,\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(4,4)\leq 1  \, \mbox{bit} \
\end{align} 
while the average gap over all possible topologies, with each topology being equally likely, is
\begin{align}
\mathbb{E}_{\mathfrak{N}}[\Delta_{\mathfrak{N}}(4,4)]\leq 0.25 \ \mbox{bits}\ .
\end{align}
<!-- end prop-5 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
\label{proof-prop_nonlinear-k-user-communication-broadcast}  

Building on Proposition~\ref{prop-3-user-gap-genie}, we now derive the gap for the given $(4,4,\{\mathcal{S}_i\}_{i\in[4]},\{f_i\}_{i\in[4]})$ non-linear broadcast model. Setting $\mathcal{S}_i = X_i$ for all $i \in [K]$ and using $q=2$, we note that
\begin{align}
\label{eq-proof-lem-4-gap-1}
I_2({\bf X}_{[N]};W\mid \mathcal{S}_{i}) 
= H_2(W\mid X_i) 
= H_2(f_i\mid X_i) + H_2(W\mid f_i, X_i) 
\end{align}
where $W$ denotes the MIS induced by $P^*_{\mathbf X_{[N]},W}$ that achieves~\eqref{eq-theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast}, and is defined in~\eqref{eq:Wopt}. 
  
Without loss of generality, assume that the maximum in~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-4-gap-1} is attained by user~$i=1$, with side information $\mathcal{S}_1 = X_1$ and demand $f_1$. Thus, adapting~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-gap-2} to the $N=K=4$ setting, we have
\begin{align}
    \label{eq-proof-lem-4-gap-2}
    H_{\kappa,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}\left(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[4]}\mid\mathcal S_{[4]}\right)= H_2(f_1\mid X_1) + H_2(W\mid f_1, X_1) \ .
\end{align}
From~\eqref{eq-genie-aided-rate}, we calculate the genie-aided lower bound as
\begin{align}
\label{eq-proof-lem-4-user-gap-genie-2}  
R^*(4,4) 
&\geq\max_{\substack{(i_1,i_2,i_3,i_4)\in \mathrm{Perm}([4])}}  \ \sum\limits_{l=1}^4 \ H_2(f_{i_l}\mid X_{i_1}, \cdots, X_{i_l}, f_{i_1}, \cdots, f_{i_{l-1}}) 
                                                                                                                                                           
                                                                                                           
                                                                                                                                                                                                            
\end{align}
where $H_2(f_{i_4}\mid X_{i_1}, X_{i_2}, X_{i_3}, X_{i_4}, f_{i_1}, f_{i_2}, f_{i_3})=0$ for all distinct $(i_1,i_2,i_3,i_4)\in \mathrm{Perm}([4])$.  

The additive gap, denoted by $\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(4,4)$, between~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-4-gap-2} and~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-4-user-gap-genie-2} is bounded as
\begin{align}
\label{eq-proof-lem-3-user-gap-genie-4}
\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(4,4)\leq  H_2(W\mid f_1, X_1) - \!\!\!\!\max\limits_{\substack{(i_2,i_3,i_4)\in \mathrm{Perm}([4]\setminus\{1\})}}\{ H_2(f_{i_2}\mid X_1, X_{i_2}, f_1) + H_2(f_{i_3}\mid X_1, X_{i_2}, X_{i_3}, f_1, f_{i_2}) \}  \ .
\end{align}



As in Proposition~\ref{prop-3-user-gap-genie}, we introduce a set of graphs to compute $\Delta(4,4)$. Consider $G_{W\mid X_1, f_1}$, obtained from $G_{\mathrm B}$ by conditioning on $(X_1,f_1)$. Conditioning on $X_1$ removes all cross edges between $\{(0000),\dots,(0111)\}$ and $\{(1000),\dots,(1111)\}$, partitioning the graph into two halves corresponding to $X_1=0$ and $X_1=1$. 
For $X_1=0$, the edges $\{(0000),(0111)\},\{(0001),(0110)\},\{(0010),(0101)\},\{(0011,0100)\}$—induced solely by $f_1$—are absent; similarly, for $X_1=1$,            
$\{(1000),(1111)\},\{(1001),(1110)\},\{(1010),(1101)\},\{(1011),(1100)\}$ are removed. Thus, no complete subgraph is induced on either half of $G_{W\mid X_1, f_1}$. The conditional entropy of $G_{W\mid X_1, f_1}$ is $H_2(W\mid X_1, f_1)$.                                                                                             

                                                                                                 

Let $\mathcal{E}_W$ denote the set of all edges in $G_{W\mid X_1, f_1}$. We classify these edges into seven groups:
\begin{align}
\mathcal{E}_1 &= \{({\bf x}_{[4]},{\bf x}_{[4]}') \in \mathcal{E}_W : \mbox{induced by } f_1\mid X_1\} ,\\
\mathcal{E}_l &= \{({\bf x}_{[4]},{\bf x}_{[4]}') \in \mathcal{E}_W : \mbox{induced by } f_1\mid X_1 \mbox{ and } f_l\mid X_l\} ,\quad l\in\{2,3,4\} ,\\
                                                                                                                                    
                                                                                                                                    
                                                                                                                                    
\mathcal{E}_{l+l'} &= \{({\bf x}_{[4]},{\bf x}_{[4]}') \in \mathcal{E}_W : \mbox{induced by } f_1\mid X_1, f_l\mid X_l, \mbox{ and } f_{l'}\mid X_{l'} \} ,\quad l,l'\in\{2,3,4\}\,\mbox{and}\, l\neq l'
                                                                                                                                                   
                                                                                                                                                   
                                                                                                                                                  
\end{align}
where ${\bf x}_{[4]}=(x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4)$ and ${\bf x}_{[4]}'=(x_1',x_2',x_3',x_4')$, and $\mathcal{E}_{W} = \cup_{i=1}^7 \mathcal{E}_i$. 


    

Now, we consider the remaining functions, namely $f_2$, $f_3$ and $f_4$, and derive a collection of graphs and their entropies by conditioning on side information, as summarized in Table~\ref{tab:Subgraphs derived from Gf-4user}.

                                   
\begin{table}[h]\small
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|}
    \hline
       & Function  & Side information & Conditional entropy & Edge class\\
         \hline
         $G_{f_2\mid X_1,X_2,f_1}$&$f_2$& $X_1,\ X_2,\ f_1$& $H_2(f_2\mid X_1,X_2,f_1)$& $\mathcal{E}_2$\\
         \hline
         $G_{f_2\mid X_1, X_2,X_3, f_1,f_3}$&$f_2$& $X_1,\ X_2,\ X_3,\ f_1,\ f_3$& $H_2(f_2\mid X_1,X_2,X_3,f_1,f_3)$& $\mathcal{E}_5$\\
         \hline
         $G_{f_2\mid X_1, X_2,X_4, f_1,f_4}$&$f_2$& $X_1, \ X_2,\ X_4,\ f_1,\ f_4$& $H_2(f_2\mid X_1,X_2,X_4,f_1,f_4)$& $\mathcal{E}_6$\\
         \hline
    \hline
         $G_{f_3\mid X_1,X_3,f_1}$&$f_3$& $X_1,\ X_3,\ f_1$& $H_2(f_3\mid X_1,X_3,f_1)$& $\mathcal{E}_3$\\
         \hline
         $G_{f_3\mid X_1, X_2,X_3, f_1,f_2}$&$f_3$& $X_1,\ X_2,\ X_3,\ f_1,\ f_2$& $H_2(f_3\mid X_1,X_2,X_3,f_1,f_2)$& $\mathcal{E}_5$\\
         \hline
         $G_{f_3\mid X_1, X_3,X_4, f_1,f_4}$&$f_3$& $X_1,\ X_3,\ X_4,\ f_1,\ f_4$& $H_2(f_3\mid X_1,X_3,X_4,f_1,f_4)$& $\mathcal{E}_7$\\
         \hline
         \hline
         $G_{f_4\mid X_1,X_4,f_1}$&$f_4$& $X_1,\ X_4,\ f_1$& $H_2(f_4\mid X_1,X_4,f_1)$& $\mathcal{E}_4$\\
         \hline
         $G_{f_4\mid X_1, X_3,X_4, f_1,f_3}$&$f_4$& $X_1,\ X_3,\ X_4,\ f_1,\ f_3$& $H_2(f_4\mid X_1,X_3,X_4,f_1,f_3)$& $\mathcal{E}_7$\\
         \hline
         $G_{f_4\mid X_1, X_2,X_4, f_1,f_2}$&$f_4$& $X_1,\ X_2,\ X_4,\ f_1,\ f_4$& $H_2(f_4\mid X_1,X_2,X_4,f_1,f_2)$& $\mathcal{E}_6$\\
         \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{\small{The $4$-user NFCB problem, corresponding to the worst-case configuration  depicted in Figure~\ref{fig:GWX1f1}, which includes the graphs for $f_2$, $f_3$, and $f_4$, each conditioned on their side information, along with their conditional entropies.}}
    \label{tab:Subgraphs derived from Gf-4user}
\end{table}

 constructing a table analogous to Tables~\ref{tab:my_label-2}-                              
\ref{table-gap-3-diff} is tedious,                      
we restrict our attention to the worst-case scenario. A worst-case instance of the $4$-user model is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:GWX1f1}. In this graph, the edges in $\mathcal{E}_1$ are removed due to knowledge of $(f_1, X_1)$, $\mathcal{E}_2$ is denoted by black solid lines, $\mathcal{E}_3$ by gray dotted lines, $\mathcal{E}_4$ by gray solid lines, $\mathcal{E}_5$ by black dashed lines, $\mathcal{E}_6$ by gray long-dashed lines, and $\mathcal{E}_7$ is denoted by black long-dashed lines. To characterize $\Delta(4,4)$, we construct a collection of graphs, obtained by conditioning on their respective side information, as shown in Figures~\ref{fig:Gf1f2_Gf1f3_Gf1f4},~\ref{fig:Gf1f2f3_Gf1f2f4_Gf1f3f2}, and~\ref{fig:Gf1f3f4_Gf1f4f2_Gf1f4f3}. The conditional entropies of these graphs are summarized in Table~\ref{tab:entropyvalues-4user}. 
We observe in Figure~\ref{fig:Gf1f2_Gf1f3_Gf1f4} (Left) that are no edges between $(x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4)\in \{0,1\}^4$ and $(x_1, x_2, x_3', x_4)\in \{0,1\}^4$ for $x_3\neq x_3'$. Hence, $H_2(f_2 \mid X_1, X_2, X_4,f_1)= 0$, and $G_{f_2\mid X_1,X_2,X_4,f_1,f_4}$ does not contain any edges. Similarly, in Figure~\ref{fig:Gf1f2_Gf1f3_Gf1f4} (Middle), there are no edges between $(x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4)\in \{0,1\}^4$ and $(x_1, x_2', x_3, x_4)\in \{0,1\}^4$. Hence, $H_2(f_3 \mid X_1, X_3, X_4,f_1) = 0$, and $G_{f_3\mid X_1,X_3,X_4,f_1,f_4}$ does not contain any edges. These observations yield
\begin{align}
    \label{eq:same_graphs}
    G_{f_4\mid X_1,X_4,f_1}\cup G_{f_2\mid X_1,X_2,X_4,f_1,f_4}=G_{f_4\mid X_1,X_4,f_1}\cup G_{f_3\mid X_1,X_3,X_4,f_1,f_4}=G_{f_4\mid X_1,X_4,f_1}
\end{align}
which can also be verified from Figures~\ref{fig:Gf1f2_Gf1f3_Gf1f4} (Right), \ref{fig:Gf1f3f4_Gf1f4f2_Gf1f4f3} (Middle), and \ref{fig:Gf1f3f4_Gf1f4f2_Gf1f4f3} (Right). In this case, we obtain
\begin{align}
\label{eq:W_cond_X1_f1}
H_2(W\mid X_1,f_1) \leq 2 \ ,\\
\label{eq:fj_cond}
H_2(f_{i_2}\mid X_1, X_{i_2}, f_1) + H_2(f_{i_3}\mid X_1, X_{i_2}, X_{i_3}, f_1, f_{i_2}) = 1 
\end{align}
for $i_2,i_3 \in [4]\setminus\{1\}$ and $i_2\ne i_3$. Plugging~\eqref{eq:W_cond_X1_f1} and~\eqref{eq:fj_cond} into~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-user-gap-genie-4}, we obtain $\max\limits_{\mathcal{N}\in \mathfrak{N}}\,\Delta_{\mathcal{N}}(4,4)\leq 1$ bit. Furthermore, the average gap over all possible topologies summarized in Tables~\ref{tab:Subgraphs derived from Gf-4user}-\ref{tab:entropyvalues-4user} --- assuming that each topology is equally likely and the gap for each half graph is upper bounded by $\Delta(3,3)$ --- satisfies $\Delta(4,4)\leq 2\Delta(3,3)=0.25$ bits.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- ex-1: body_md -->
\label{ex-nonlinear-comp}
Consider a NFCB model $(3,3,\{X_i\}_{i\in[3]},\{f_i\}_{i\in[3]})$, where elements of ${\bf X}_{[3]}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{F}_2^3)$, and the desired functions are distinct and given as
\begin{align}
\label{eq:desired_functions_example1}
f_1=X_1\wedge X_2\wedge X_3 \ ,\quad
f_2=X_1\vee X_2\vee X_3 \ ,\quad  f_3=X_1\wedge(X_2\vee X_3) \ .
\end{align}

Below, we present three approaches to design $M$: 

                                
{\bf i) Slepian-Wolf coding approach.} 
Employing the Slepian-Wolf theorem~\cite{SlepWolf1973}, the minimum broadcast rate that allows for asymptotically lossless reconstruction of $\{X_j\}_{j\in[3]}$ at all $K=3$ users is given as 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:rate_SW_ex1}
R_{\mathrm{SW}}(3,3)=\max\limits_{i\in[3]}\,  H_2({\bf X}_{[3]\backslash i})=2\ \mbox{bits} 
\end{align}
which can be achieved through transmissions $X_1\oplus_2 X_2$ and $X_2\oplus_2 X_3$. Each user can recover $\{X_j\}_{j\in[3]}$ using $X_1\oplus_2 X_2$ and $X_2\oplus_2 X_3$ as well as their side information.






                                                    
{\bf ii) Broadcast graph approach.} 
We next employ the broadcast graph approach (cf. Definition~\ref{def:broadcast_graph}), where $G_{f_1}=(\mathbb{F}_2^3,\mathcal{E}_1)$ given $\mathcal{S}_1=X_1$ is shown in Figure~\ref{fig:Graphs_ex1}-$(\mathrm{a})$. In $G_{f_1}$, we do not need to distinguish the vertices with $X_1=0$ because the outcome of $f_1 =0$ for any $(x_2,x_3)\in\mathbb{F}_2^2$. When $X_1=1$, $f_1 =1$ if and only if $X_2=X_3=1$, and otherwise $f_1=0$. Thus, $\mathcal{E}_1=\{(111,100), (111,101),(111,110)\}$. Hence, 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:example1_LB1}
H_\chi(G_{f_1},{\bf X}_{[N]}\mid X_1)\triangleq\min\limits_{\substack{P_{U_1\mid{\bf X}_{[3]}}:\\{\bf X}_{[3]}\in U_1\overset{\circ}{\in}\Gamma(G_{f_1})}}\, I_2({\bf X}_{[3]};U_1\mid X_1)=H_2(U_1\mid X_1)                                                                 
=H_2(f_1\mid X_1)=0.406 \ \mbox{bits}
\end{align}
noting that $U_1\in\{\{111\},\mathcal{X}_{[3]}\backslash\{111\}\}$, and $U_1\overset{\circ}{\in}\Gamma(G_{f_1})$~\cite{alon1996source}, i.e., $H_2(U_1\mid {\bf X}_{[3]})=0$. 

Similarly, we construct $G_{f_2}$ and $G_{f_3}$, as shown in Figure~\ref{fig:Graphs_ex1}-$(\mathrm{b})$ and $(\mathrm{c})$, respectively. Furthermore, 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:example1_LB2}
H_\chi(G_{f_2},{\bf X}_{[N]}\mid X_2)=H_2(U_2\mid X_2)                                   
=H_2(f_2\mid X_2)=0.406 \ \mbox{bits}
\end{align}
noting that $U_2\in\{\{000\},\mathcal{X}_{[3]}\backslash\{000\}\}$. 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:example1_LB3}
H_\chi(G_{f_3},{\bf X}_{[N]}\mid X_3)=H_2(U_3\mid X_3)                                               
=H_2(f_3\mid X_3)=0.906 \ \mbox{bits}
\end{align}
noting that $U_3\in\{\{000,001,010,011,100\},\{101,110,111\}\}$. 

A universal lower bound, using~\eqref{eq:example1_LB1},~\eqref{eq:example1_LB2}, and~\eqref{eq:example1_LB3}, is given by 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:example1_lowerbound}
R^*(3,3)\geq \max\limits_{i\in[3]}\, H_\chi(G_{f_i},{\bf X}_{[N]}\mid X_i) =H_2(f_3\mid X_3)=0.906 \ \mbox{bits}
\end{align}
where the inequality can be strict as functions are not compatible (see Theorem~\ref{theo-lower-bound-mutual-info}). 

The broadcast graph, $G_{\mathrm B}$, which captures $(\{\mathcal{S}_i\}_{i\in[3]},\ \{f_i\}_{i\in[3]})$, is shown in Figure~\ref{fig:Graphs_ex1}~$(\mathrm{d})$. Its independence number is $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})=4$, and it has the following set of MISs:
 \begin{align}
 \label{Gamma-3-user}
\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})=\left\{\begin{array}{c}
       W_1=\{101,110\} \ ,
       W_2=\{000,010,011\} \ ,\ W_3=\{000,010,111\} \ , \, \\
       W_4=\{001,011,110\}\ ,\ W_5=\{ 010,100,101\} \ ,
       W_6=\{001,010,011,100\} 
  \end{array}\right\} \ .
  \end{align}


The achievable broadcast rate, employing~\eqref{eq-theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast} from Theorem~\ref{theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast}, is given by 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:mutual_info_ex1}
H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=I_2({\bf X}_{[3]};W)=1.417 \ \mbox{bits}
\end{align}
where the conditional distribution $P^*_{W\mid \mathbf X_{[3]}}$ that achieves the optimum~\eqref{eq:mutual_info_ex1} is illustrated in Table~\ref{tab:optimal-joint-distribution}.
