{\bf iii) Minimum entropy coloring-based approach.} 
From the sandwich property of $\operatorname{minrk}$~\cite{bar2011index}, $\chi(G_{\mathrm B})=\alpha(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B})=3$.                             
                                                                                                              
Thus, the optimal one-shot zero-error rate is 
\begin{align}
R_{1,0}^*(3,3)=\log_2 \chi(G_{\mathrm B})=\log_2 3=1.585 \, \mbox{bits}.
\end{align}


Let $C=c_{G_{\mathrm B}}(\mathbf X_{[3]})\in\{R,B,G\}$ denote the color assigned to the source realization, where
\begin{align}
c_{G_{\mathrm B}}^{-1}(R)=\{000,111\},\quad
c_{G_{\mathrm B}}^{-1}(B)=\{001,010,011,100\},\quad
c_{G_{\mathrm B}}^{-1}(G)=\{101,110\} .
\end{align}
Under $\mathbf X_{[3]}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb F_2^3)$, its unconditional distribution is $\bigl(\mathbb{P}(C=R),\mathbb{P}(C=B),\mathbb{P}(C=G)\bigr)=\left(0.25,0.5,0.25\right)$. 
Conditioned on the $i$-th source coordinate, the color distributions are
\begin{align}
\bigl(\mathbb{P}(C=R\mid X_1=0),\mathbb{P}(C=B\mid X_1=0),
\mathbb{P}(C=G\mid X_1=0)\bigr)
&=\left(0.25,0.75,0\right),\\
\bigl(\mathbb{P}(C=R\mid X_1=1),\mathbb{P}(C=B\mid X_1=1),
\mathbb{P}(C=G\mid X_1=1)\bigr)
&=\left(0.25,0.25,0.5\right),\\
\bigl(\mathbb{P}(C=R\mid X_i=x_i),\mathbb{P}(C=B\mid X_i=x_i),\mathbb{P}(C=G\mid X_i=x_i)\bigr)&=\left(0.25,0.5,0.25\right)
\end{align}
for every $i\in\{2,3\}$ and $x_i\in\{0,1\}$. Thus,
\begin{align}
H_2(C\mid X_1)&=\frac12 \mathsf H_2((0.25,0.75,0))+\frac12 \mathsf H_2((0.25,0.25,0.5))\approx 1.156 \ \text{bits},\\
H_2(C\mid X_2)&=H_2(C\mid X_3)=1.5 \ \text{bits}.
\end{align}
For this deterministic coloring, one can equivalently define $W=c_{G_{\mathrm B}}^{-1}(C)$, in which case $H_2(W\mid X_i)=H_2(C\mid X_i)$. Therefore, the conditional rate associated with this coloring is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:conditional_color_entropy}
\max_{i\in[3]}H_2(C\mid X_i)=H_2(W)=1.5 \ \text{bits}.
\end{align}

 
A proper minimum entropy coloring of $G_{\mathrm B}$ is achieved using a coloring   $c_{G_{\mathrm B}}\in\{R,B,G\}$ that satisfies $\mathbb{P}(R)=\mathbb{P}(W=\{000,111 \})=0.25$, $\mathbb{P}(B)=\mathbb{P}(W=\{001,010,011,100\})=0.5$, and $\mathbb{P}(G)=\mathbb{P}(W=\{101,110\})=0.25$ (see Figure~\ref{fig:ex1_coloring_and_its_complement} (Left) for $G_{\mathrm B}$). Hence, from~\eqref{eq:chromatic_entropy_vs_graph_entropy}
\begin{align}
\label{eq:rate_minentropy_ex1}
H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})&\overset{(a)}{=} \min\limits_{\substack{P_{W\mid{\bf X}_{[3]}}:\\{\bf X}_{[3]}\in W,\;W\overset{\circ}{\in}\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})}}\, H_3(W) \nonumber\\
&\overset{(b)}{=} H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid \mathcal S_{[3]}=\mathbf X_{[3]})                                                               
=H_2(W)=\mathsf H_2((0.25,0.5,0.25))=1.5 \, \mbox{bits}\nonumber\\
&\overset{(c)}{>} \max_{i\in [3]}\, H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]}\mid X_i)=1.406 \, \mbox{bits}
\end{align}
where $(a)$ holds since $W\overset{\circ}{\in}\Gamma$~\cite{alon1996source}, and equality in $(b)$ is specific to this example (~\eqref{eq:conditional_color_entropy}) and may not hold in general because $H_2(W)\geq H_2(W\mid X_i)$, for all $i\in[K]$, and step $(c)$ follows from~\eqref{eq:Rate_ach}, which is evaluated numerically. The solution in~\eqref{eq:rate_minentropy_ex1}, $H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})$ is not optimal, and  can be tightened using $G^{\dot{\vee} n}_{\mathrm B}$                                              
. Sending colorings of  $G^{\dot{\vee}n}_{\mathrm B}$ for sufficiently large $n$, followed by Slepian-Wolf coding, yields an achievable coding scheme (cf.~\cite{korner1971coding} and~\cite{feizi2014network}). However, the lower bound is not necessarily achievable as $\{f_i\}$ are not compatible (Propositions~\ref{prop_achievable-rate-via-broadcast_compatible} and~\ref{prop-2-user-rate_optimality}).

For the given $G_{\mathrm B}$, the number of maximal cliques is $6$, of which five are triangles, and one is a maximal edge, and the set of cliques is given as
\begin{align}
\label{eq:Omega-3-user}
\Omega(G_{\mathrm B})=\left\{\begin{array}{c}
W'_1=\{000,001,101\} \ , \          
W'_2=\{000,100,110\} \ , \         
W'_3=\{001,101,111\} \ , \\         
W'_4=\{011,101,111\} \ , \         
W'_5=\{100,110,111\} \ , \          
W'_6=\{010,110\}       
\end{array}\right\} .
\end{align}
The clique number $\omega(G_{\mathrm B})$ is the size of the largest clique in $G_{\mathrm B}$. Noting that there is no clique with more than three vertices, is $\omega(G_{\mathrm B})=\alpha(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B})=3$ (see  Figure~\ref{fig:ex1_coloring_and_its_complement} (Right) for $\overline{G}_{\mathrm B}$). 
 
We further note from~\eqref{eq:clique_entropy} that
\begin{align}
\label{eq:clique_entropy_ex1}
H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=H_2({\bf X}_{[3]}\mid W^')=1.417 \, \mbox{bits} 
\end{align}
where $W^'$ denotes the clique-set-valued random variable induced by $P^*_{\mathbf X_{[N]},W^'}=P_{\mathbf X_{[N]}}P^*_{W^'\mid{\mathbf X}_{[N]}}$. An optimal conditional distribution $P^*_{W^'\mid \mathbf X_{[3]}}$ that achieves~\eqref{eq:clique_entropy_ex1} is given in Table~\ref{tab:optimal-joint-distribution_clique}.

             \hline &&&&&&&&\\[-1em]
             $W'_1=\{000, 001, 101\}$&$0.43$&$0.664$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0.332$&$0$&$0$\\[0.1em]
             \hline&&&&&&&&\\[-1em]
             $W'_2=\{000, 100, 110\}$&$0.57$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0.809$&$0$&$0.51$&$0$  \\

Moreover, neither $G_{\mathrm B}$ nor its complement $\overline{G}_{\mathrm B}$ contains an induced odd cycle of length at least five\footnote{                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    
                                                                            
From the Strong Perfect Graph Theorem, $G_{\mathrm B}$ is perfect if no induced subgraph of $G_{\mathrm B}$ is an odd cycle of length at least five (that is $C_{2k+1}$, $k\geq 2$) or the complement of one~\cite{berge1961farbung}.}. Since $G_{\mathrm B}$ has eight vertices, it suffices to verify the absence of induced cycles of length five and seven in both $G_{\mathrm B}$ and $\overline{G}_{\mathrm B}$. Hence, by the Strong Perfect Graph Theorem, $G_{\mathrm B}$ is perfect\footnote{A perfect graph $G$ is a graph in which for every induced subgraph $G'\subseteq G$,                                                                                                                                                                                                       
$\omega(G')=\chi(G')$~\cite{hoang1987conjecture}.}~\cite{hoang1987conjecture}. Therefore, from~\cite{csiszar1990entropy} and noting that $\mathbf X_{[3]}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb F_2^3)$
\begin{align}
\label{eq:perfect-graph-entropy-identity}
H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})+H_\kappa(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=H_2(\mathbf X_{[3]})=3 \, \mbox{bits} .
\end{align} 
From~\cite[Lemma~12]{alon1996source}, $H_\omega(G,X)=H_q(X)-H_\kappa(\overline{G},X)$ for every probabilistic graph $G$. Thus, $H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=3-H_\kappa(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},{\mathbf X}_{[3]})=1.417$ bits, verifying~\eqref{eq:clique_entropy_ex1}. 
The optimal zero-error broadcast rate is (see~\eqref{eq:asymptotic_zero_error_clique_bound})
\begin{align}
\label{eq:asymptotic_zero_error_clique_bound_Ex1}
R_0^*(3,3)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^n\right)\neq H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}) , 
\end{align} 
in general, because perfectness of $G_{\mathrm B}$ does not ensure that the exact operational block graph $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$ is perfect, and $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}\neq G^{\dot{\vee} n}_{\mathrm B}$ in general, perfectness of the single-letter graph $G_{\mathrm B}$ alone does not establish a single-letter operational converse. 
 
Furthermore, the clique quantity for $G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)}$ satisfies $\frac12 H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^2)=1.364$ bits, for $G_{\mathrm B}^{(3)}$, it holds that $\frac13 H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(3)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^3)\gtrsim 1.301$, and for $G_{\mathrm B}^{(4)}$, we have $\frac14 H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(4)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^4)\gtrsim 1.234$,  showing that the single-letter clique entropy $H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=1.417$ bits does not tensorize, and does not provide a matching operational characterization.                                                                                                       
Similarly, employing Lemma~\ref{lem:smoothing_lemma} yields the same lower bound for $R^*(3,3)$ as in~\eqref{eq:asymptotic_zero_error_clique_bound_Ex1}.

Proposition~\ref{prop-genie}, from $H_2(f_3\mid X_3)=0.905$, $H_2(f_1\mid X_1, X_3, f_3)=0.25$, and $H_2(f_2 \mid X_2,X_1,X_3, f_1,f_3)=0$, yields 
 \begin{align}
 \label{eq-low-bound-genie-ex-3-user-nonlin}
 R^*(3,3) \geq H_2(f_3\mid X_3)+H_2(f_1\mid X_1, X_3, f_3) + H_2(f_2 \mid X_2,X_1,X_3, f_1,f_3) =1.155\, \mbox{ bits}\ .
 \end{align}
 Contrasting~\eqref{eq:rate_minentropy_ex1}                                                   
with~\eqref{eq-low-bound-genie-ex-3-user-nonlin} showcases the tightness of the former bound                                              
versus Proposition~\ref{prop-genie}.
 



Contrasting the three schemes outlined above, we obtain the following. 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:rate_comparison_ex1}
1.155\leq R^*(3,3)\leq R_0^*(3,3)\leq H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=1.417\leq  H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})=1.5 \leq R_{\mathrm{SW}}(3,3)=2 .
\end{align}
<!-- end ex-1 -->
<!-- ex-2: body_md -->
\label{ex-linear-comp-jafar} 
Consider a $(3,3,\{X_i\}_{i\in[3]},$ $\{\mathbf{X}_{[3]}^{\top}\mathbf{V}_i\}_{i\in[3]})$ linear computation broadcast model, where ${\bf X}_{[3]}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{F}_3^3)$, and the desired functions are 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:desired_functions_example2}
f_1=\mathbf{X}_{[3]}^{\top}\mathbf{V}_1 ,\quad
f_2=\mathbf{X}_{[3]}^{\top}\mathbf{V}_2 ,\quad
f_3=\mathbf{X}_{[3]}^{\top}\mathbf{V}_3 
\end{align}
where $\mathbf{V}_1^{\intercal}=\small{\begin{bmatrix}0 & 1& 1\end{bmatrix}}$, $\mathbf{V}_2^{\intercal}=\small{\begin{bmatrix}1 & 0& 1\end{bmatrix}}$, and $\mathbf{V}_3^{\intercal}=\small{\begin{bmatrix}1 & 2& 0\end{bmatrix}}$. 

Below, we present three approaches to design $M$: 

                          
{\bf i) MinRank approach.} 
Given the set of functions in~\eqref{eq:desired_functions_example2}, the vector $\mathbf{V}_i$ represents the coefficients of $f_i$, $i\in[3]$,  and each element $\mathbf{V}_i(j)$ for $j\in[3]$ corresponds to the coefficients assigned to $X_j\in\mathbb{F}_q$. 

Employing~\eqref{eq-min-rank-linear-demand}                                      
yields the following optimal linear vector index code length: 
\begin{align}
\label{eq-example-matrix-3-user-linear}
\operatorname{minrk}_q(\{{\bf V}_i, {\bf V}_i'\}_{i\in[3]})=\min_{\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3}\, \left\{\operatorname{{\rm rk}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}
    \lambda_{1} & 1 & 1 \\
    1 & \lambda_{2} & 1\\
    1 & 2 & \lambda_{3} 
\end{bmatrix} \right) \ : \ \lambda_i\in\mathbb{F}_3,\ \forall\, i\in[3]\right\}=2         
                                                                                                                                                                                                                           
\end{align}
                                                                                                                                                                                                                             
which follows from noting that ${\bf V}_1^{\prime\intercal}=\small{\begin{bmatrix}1 & 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}}$, ${\bf V}_2^{\prime\intercal}=\small{\begin{bmatrix}0 & 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}}$, and ${\bf V}_3^{\prime\intercal}=\small{\begin{bmatrix}0 & 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}}$, respectively, because the side information of each user is $X_{i}={\bf X}_{[3]}^\intercal {\bf V}_{i}^{\prime}$ for $i\in [3]$. 
 
The optimal solution of~\eqref{eq-example-matrix-3-user-linear} is achieved by letting 
$\lambda_1^*=0$, $\lambda_2^*=0$, and $\lambda_3^*=2$,                                                  
noting that the last row of the matrix in~\eqref{eq-example-matrix-3-user-linear} is a linear combination of the first two rows. The optimal index code with length $2$ is achievable when the sender node broadcasts
\begin{align}
\label{Aopt_Example3_standard}
{\bf W}^*=\begin{bmatrix}
    0 & 1 & 1 \\
    1 & 0 & 1
\end{bmatrix}{\bf X}_{[3]}^{\intercal} \ .
\end{align}
                                           


                                           

                          
{\bf ii) Compatibility function-based approach.}  
Building on the compatibility function–based approach of~\cite{yao2024capacity, ravi2016broadcast},                                                                                                                                                                                                                         
we next employ a data-splitting approach to construct pairwise compatible transmissions and derive an upper bound on the total rate required to satisfy all users' demands.

Given user pair $(1,2)$, with demands given above, a compatible function is $Z_{12}=X_1\oplus_{3} X_2\oplus_{3} X_3$. Similarly, for user pairs $(1,3)$, and $(2,3)$,  functions $Z_{13}=X_1\oplus_{3} 2 X_2\oplus_{3} 2X_3$ and $Z_{23}=X_1\oplus_{3} 2X_2\oplus_{3} X_3$, respectively, are compatible. For any pair of distinct users $\{i_1,i_2\}$, the rate achieved by $Z_{{i_1}{i_2}}$ satisfies
\begin{align}
    \label{eq:Rij-constraint}
    R_{i_1 i_2}\leq H_3(Z_{{i_1}{i_2}})=H_3(f_{i_1}\mid X_{i_1})=1 \, \mbox{bit}, \quad 
    \{i_1,i_2\}\in[3]\ : \ i_1\neq i_2 \ .
\end{align}
 

Employing the data-splitting idea of~\cite[Example~2]{yao2024capacity}, we split each $X_j$, $j\in[3]$ into two disjoint sub-variables $X_j^l$, $l\in[2]$.                                  
Similarly, let $Z_{{i_1}{i_2}}^l$,  $l\in[2]$, denote the sub-variables for the compatible functions defined above, with $H_3(Z_{{i_1}{i_2}}^l)=0.5$ bits each. Consider the broadcast messages: (i) $Z_{12}^1$, (ii) $Z_{23}^2$, and (iii) $Z_{13}^1 \oplus_3 Z_{13}^2$. From (i) and (iii), $f_1$ is decoded; using (i) and (ii), $f_2$ is decoded; and finally from (ii) and (iii), $f_3$ is decoded. Thus, splitting each dataset into two subsymbols followed by jointly encoding the two instances,                                   
achieves the broadcast rate  
\begin{align}
 \label{ex-linear-rate-split}
 R_{\mathrm Compat.}(3,3)&= H_3(Z_{12}^1,Z_{23}^2,Z_{13}^1\oplus_{3}Z_{13}^{2})\nonumber\\
 &\overset{(a)}{=}H_3(Z_{12}^1)+H_{3}(Z_{23}^{2}\mid Z_{12}^1)+H_{3}(Z_{13}^1\oplus_{3}Z_{13}^{2}\mid Z_{12}^1, Z_{23}^{2})\nonumber\\
 &\overset{(b)}{=} H_3(Z_{12}^1)+H_{3}(Z_{23}^{2})+H_{3}(Z_{13}^1\oplus_{3}Z_{13}^{2})=1.5\,\mbox{bits} 
 \end{align}
where $(a)$ follows from the chain rule, and $(b)$ from                                                
the independence of $Z_{12}^1$, $Z_{23}^2$, and noting that $H_{3}(Z_{13}^1\oplus_{3}Z_{13}^{2}\mid Z_{12}^1, Z_{23}^{2})=H_{3}(X_1^1\oplus_{3}2X_2^1\oplus_{3}2X_3^1\oplus_{3}X_1^2\oplus_{3}2X_2^2\oplus_{3}2X_3^2\mid X_1^1\oplus_3 X_2^1\oplus_3 X_3^1, X_1^2\oplus_3 2X_2^2\oplus_3 X_3^2)=H_3(X_2^1\oplus_{3}X_3^1\oplus_{3}X_3^2\mid X_1^1\oplus_3 X_2^1\oplus_3 X_3^1, X_1^2\oplus_3 2X_2^2\oplus_3 X_3^2)=0.5$.



                                           
                          
{\bf iii) Broadcast graph approach.} 
We next exploit the achievability result in                                                          
Proposition~\ref{prop_achievable-rate-via-broadcast_compatible} for the linear function computation broadcast setting in this example. The sets of independent sets of each $G_{f_i}$, $i\in[3]$, are given as
\begin{align}
\label{Gamma-3-user-linear-separate}
\Gamma(G_{f_1})&=
\{\{\star 00,\star12,\star21\},\{\star01,\star10,\star22\},\{\star02,\star20,\star11\}\} \ ,\\
\Gamma(G_{f_2})&=\{ \{0\star0,1\star2,2\star1\},\{0\star1,1\star0,2\star2\},\{0\star2,2\star0,1\star1\}\}\ , \\ 
\Gamma(G_{f_3})&=\{\{00\star,11\star,22\star\}, \{02\star,10\star,21\star\},\{01\star,12\star,20\star\}\}
\end{align}
where $\star$'s denote don't care terms, i.e., values in  $\star$ positions may be chosen arbitrarily without altering the outcome. 

The resulting broadcast graph, $G_{\mathrm B}=(\mathbb F_3^3,\mathcal E_{\mathrm B})$, with $|\mathcal{V}(G_{\mathrm B})|=27$, can be derived from $(\{\mathcal{S}_i\}_{i\in[3]},\ \{f_i\}_{i\in[3]})$. Employing the method of~\cite{tsukiyama1977new}, we enumerated all MISs of a graph, and numerically\footnote{The implementation is available at \href{https://github.com/kumarkpvijith/Non-linear-function-computation-broadcast.git}{https://github.com/kumarkpvijith/Non-linear-function-computation-broadcast.git}.}, and verified that $G_{\mathrm B}$ has $225$ MISs. Table~\ref{tab:w-lin} presents only those MISs with nonzero probabilities that optimize $H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})$ (see~\eqref{eq:chromatic_entropy_vs_graph_entropy}). 
The sender can then exploit Slepian-Wolf coding to encode $W^n$ against the side information $X_i^n$ simultaneously for all three users. Thus, random binning at any rate $R>\max\limits_{i\in[3]}H_3(W\mid X_i)=H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid \mathcal S_{[3]}=\mathbf X_{[3]})                                                                     
$ allows all users to recover $W^n$ with probability of error tending to zero. Hence, the side-information-assisted rate                            
is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:ach_MinRank_Example2}
R^*(3,3)\leq H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid \mathcal S_{[3]}=\mathbf X_{[3]})                                                                      
=1.72 \   \mbox{trits}  
\end{align}
where $P^*_W$ given in Table~\ref{tab:w-lin} that achieves~\eqref{eq:chromatic_entropy_vs_graph_entropy} assigns positive probabilities to disjoint MISs $\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$. 




                                        
\begin{table}[t!]\footnotesize
\centering
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{1.2}{1.2}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c||c|c|}
\hline
$W$ & $\mathbb{P}(W)$ & $W$ & $\mathbb{P}(W)$ \\
\hline
$W_1=\{000,012,121,201\}$ & $\frac{4}{27}$ & $W_5=\{021,101,200,212\}$ & $\frac{4}{27}$ \\
$W_2=\{001,010,120,222\}$ & $\frac{4}{27}$ & $W_6=\{022,102,111,210\}$ & $\frac{4}{27}$ \\
$W_3=\{002,011,110,220\}$ & $\frac{4}{27}$ & $W_7=\{122,202,211\}$ & $\frac{3}{27}$ \\
$W_4=\{020,100,112,221\}$ & $\frac{4}{27}$ & & \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  
\caption{\small Distribution $P^*_W$ that optimizes $H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})$ (see~\eqref{eq:chromatic_entropy_vs_graph_entropy}) (cf. Example~\ref{ex-linear-comp-jafar}).}
\label{tab:w-lin}
\end{table}
                                      



$G_{\mathrm B}$ has $|\mathcal{V}|=27$, $\mathrm{deg}(v)=12$ for every $v\in\mathcal{V}$ and is vertex-transitive\footnote{A vertex-transitive graph is a graph where every vertex looks identical from a structural perspective, meaning that for any two vertices, there is an automorphism (a symmetry-preserving map) that moves one vertex to the other~\cite{godsil2001algebraic}.}. For a vertex-transitive graph $G$ under uniform distribution, $H_\kappa(G,X)\triangleq\log\frac{|\mathcal{V}(G)|}{\alpha(G)}$. Thus, from Theorem~\ref{theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast}, the unconditional K\"orner graph-entropy is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:VT_graph_entropy}
H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=\log_3 \frac{27}{4}=1.738 \   \mbox{trits}  
\end{align}
which does not exploit each user's side information, unlike the $1.72$ ternary symbols scheme.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              


Because $G_{\mathrm B}$ is vertex-transitive and $\alpha(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B})=\omega(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B})=4$, and $\overline{G}_{\mathrm B}$ is also vertex-transitive, i.e., $H_\kappa(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=1.738 \ \mbox{trits}$.                                                                                                                                                                          
Furthermore, $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})=\omega(G_{\mathrm B})=4$ and $\chi(G_{\mathrm B})=7$. Thus, $G_{\mathrm B}$ is not perfect. From~\cite[Lemma~12]{alon1996source}, $H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=3-H_\kappa(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=1.262<H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=1.738$ trits.

From~\eqref{eq:ach_MinRank_Example2} (see Table~\ref{tab:w-lin}) and~\eqref{eq:VT_graph_entropy}, the asymptotically lossless functional-computation rate satisfies
\begin{align}
R^*(3,3)\leq \min\Bigl\{H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid \mathcal S_{[3]}=\mathbf X_{[3]})                                                                      
,\ H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})\Bigr\}= 1.72 \ \mbox{trits} .
\end{align}


The single-letter broadcast graph $G_{\mathrm B}$ has $225$ MISs, of which $162$ have cardinality four and $63$ have cardinality three. Solving the resulting convex minimax problem yields $H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid X_i)=1.667$ trits.                                     
Adopting the definition~\eqref{eq:single_letter_side_info_assisted_def_G_B}, where, numerically, the three conditional mutual informations are equal at the optimum:
\begin{align}
H_{\kappa,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}\left(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid\mathcal S_{[3]}\right)=I_3({\bf X}_{[3]};W\mid X_i)=1.667 \ \mbox{trits},\quad i\in[3]
\end{align}
where $W\in\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$. Applying this construction repeatedly treats two sequences as requiring separation whenever $\exists t\in[n],\exists i\in[3]:\ X_{i,t}=\widetilde X_{i,t},\ f_i(\mathbf x_{[N],t})\neq\widetilde f_i(\widetilde{\bf x}_{[N],t})$ where the witnessing user $i\in[3]$ may change with $t$.

Applying the genie-aided lower bound of Proposition~\ref{prop-genie} yields~\eqref{eq-proof-lem-3-user-gap-genie-2}, which gives $R^*(3,3)\geq 1$, and is not tight. 

The operational block graph $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}=\left(\mathbb F_3^{3n},\mathcal E_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}\right)$ for this example is a Cayley graph because adjacency depends only on the difference between two source blocks~\cite{magnus2004combinatorial}. Furthermore, $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$ is vertex-transitive. This gives
\begin{align}
\label{eq:Cayley_group_cosets}
\frac1n\log_3\frac{3^{3n}}{\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})}\overset{(a)}{\leq} \frac1n H_{\chi,\epsilon}\Bigl(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^n\Bigr)\overset{(b)}{\leq} \frac1nH_3(c_n(\mathbf X_{[3]}^n)) 
\end{align}
for a proper coloring $c_n\in\mathscr C(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})$, where $(a)$ holds because $\mathbf X_{[3]}^n\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb F_3^{3n})$, the graph has $|\mathcal{V}(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})|=3^{3n}$ equally likely vertices, and because every color class of a proper coloring is an independent set, its cardinality cannot exceed $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})$. Thus, the largest color probability is at most $\frac{\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})}{3^{3n}}$. Because this holds for every proper coloring, for the minimum-entropy coloring, $H_{\chi,\epsilon}\Bigl(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^n\Bigr)\geq \log_3\frac{3^{3n}}{\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})}$. 
Inequality $(b)$ holds by~\eqref{eq:operational-chromatic-entropy}. 






\paragraph{K\"orner graph entropy of block Cayley graphs}

Since ${\bf X}_{[3]}^n\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb F_3^{3n})$, the normalized graph entropy of a vertex-transitive graph satisfies
\begin{align}
\label{eq:normalized-korner-cayley-example-2}
\frac1n H_\kappa\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[3]}^n\right)=\frac1n\log_3\frac{|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})|}{\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})}=3-\frac1n\log_3\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})
\end{align}
which is determined by the exponential growth rate of the independence number.


For $n=2$, $H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^2)=\log_3 \frac{|\mathcal{V}(G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)})|}{\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)})}\leq 3$ by employing $|\mathcal{V}(G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)})|=3^6=729$ and $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)})\geq 27$ because each color class contains $3^{6-3}=27$ vertices and every coset is an independent set of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)}$ due to the zero error property. 
The $27$-color splitting construction yields the exact two-letter result $\frac12 H_{\chi,\epsilon}\Bigl(G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^2\Bigr)=1.5$ trits. Together with the $27$-color splitting construction, we obtain the exact-two letter result $\frac12 H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^2)\leq 1.5$ trits. This scheme, similar to the splitting scheme detailed in part ii), only separates pairs that are genuinely confusable in $G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)}$ where $G_{\mathrm B}^{(2)}\subsetneq G_{\mathrm B}^{\dot\vee 2}$. 
                                                                                                
For $n=3$, the known splitting construction uses five ternary transmissions, giving rate $1.667$ trits. Thus, $\frac13 H_{\chi,\epsilon}\Bigl(G_{\mathrm B}^{(3)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^3\Bigr)\leq 1.667$ trits. Furthermore, $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(3)})\geq 108$, where the construction on the RHS is obtained by concatenating an independent set of size $27$ for $n=2$ and an independent set of size $4$ for $n=1$. Thus, $H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B}^{(3)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^3)\leq \log_3 \frac{3^9}{108}$, yielding $\frac13H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B}^{(3)},\mathbf X_{[3]}^3)\leq 1.58$ trits. For $n>3$, we can upper bound the graph entropy, as detailed next.


\begin{comment}
                          
                                                                                                                                
             
                              
           
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 

     
             
                                                                                                               
                                    
           
                                                                                                              
             
                                   
                                                                                                                                                                                      
           
                                         
             
                                   
                                                                                                                                                         
        
           
\end{comment}

\paragraph{Upper bound induced by the linear construction and the unrestricted K\"orner graph entropy construction}
Denoting by $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})$, and $\alpha_{\mathrm{lin}}(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})$ unrestricted and linear independence numbers, respectively, it follows that 
                                                                                                                                                      
\begin{multline}
\label{eq:unrestricted_vs_linear}
\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})=\max\left\{|\mathcal I|:\mathcal I\subseteq \mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})\subseteq\mathbb F_3^{3n},\ \mathcal I\text{ is independent}\right\}\\
\geq \alpha_{\mathrm{lin}}(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})\triangleq\max\left\{|\mathcal K|:\mathcal K\leq\mathbb F_3^{3n},\ \mathcal K\text{ is an independent linear subspace}\right\}=3^{\lfloor1.5n\rfloor} 
\end{multline}
where $K\leq \mathbb F_3^{3n}$ means that $K$ is a linear subspace of $\mathbb F_3^{3n}$.                                                                                         
The linear splitting construction (part ii)) produces an independent subspace of dimension $\left\lfloor1.5n\right\rfloor$. Thus,                                                                       
the achievable rate for linear encoding is
\begin{align}
\label{eq:normalized-korner-linear-upper}
R_n^{\mathrm{lin}}= 3-\frac1n\log_3(\alpha_{\mathrm{lin}}(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}))=\lceil1.5n\rceil n^{-1}\leq 1.5+0.5n^{-1} ,
\end{align}
where the even-blocklength subsequence is bounded above by $1.5$, while the odd-blocklength upper bound approaches $1.5$ from above. 
Equality in~\eqref{eq:normalized-korner-linear-upper} would require proving $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})=3^{\lfloor1.5n\rfloor}$. The linear kernel argument establishes that no larger linearly independent subspace exists, but it does not exclude larger non-linear independent sets.

From~\eqref{eq:normalized-korner-cayley-example-2} and~\eqref{eq:unrestricted_vs_linear}, 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:normalized-korner-upper}
\frac1n H_\kappa\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[3]}^n\right)=3-\frac1n\log_3(\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}))\leq 1.5+0.238n^{-1}
\end{align}
where for $n=2\ell$, $\ell\in\mathbb Z$, $3-\lfloor1.5n\rfloor n^{-1}=1.5$, and because all coordinates can be paired and each pair contributes an independent set of size $27$, we have $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})\geq 27^\ell$. Hence, $3-\frac1n\log_3(\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}))\leq 3-1.5=1.5$. 
For $n=2\ell+1$, $\ell\in\mathbb Z$, only $2\ell=n-1$ coordinates can be paired, and one remains unpaired, where the paired coordinates contribute $27^\ell$, while the remaining one-letter coordinate contributes an independent set of size $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(1)})=4$. Thus, $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})\geq 4\cdot 27^\ell$. Hence, $3-\frac1n\log_3(\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}))\leq 3-\frac1n(3\ell+\log_3 4)=1.5+(1.5-\log_3 4)n^{-1}$. 
Upper-bounding these two cases and noting that $1.5-\log_3 4=0.238$, the inequality in~\eqref{eq:normalized-korner-upper} follows.

\begin{comment}
                                 
              
                      
                                                                                                          
  
      
                         
                               
                               
                               
                               
                             
             
            
\end{comment}


\paragraph{A general lower bound}
Employing the clique--coclique bound, which gives $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})\leq|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})|$,  and exploiting~\eqref{eq:normalized-korner-cayley-example-2} yield
\begin{align}
\label{eq:clique_coclique_vertex_transitive_ex2}
H_\kappa\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[3]}^n\right)\geq \log_3 \omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}) .
\end{align}

Furthermore, since $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$ is Cayley, using~\cite{conlon2024clique}, the clique number exponent  equals $1$,
              
                               
                                                                
            
\begin{align} 
\label{eq:clique_no_limit_ex2}
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log_3\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})=1 .
\end{align}

Combining the clique lower bound~\eqref{eq:clique_coclique_vertex_transitive_ex2} and the linear upper bound~\eqref{eq:normalized-korner-linear-upper} gives
\begin{align}
\label{eq:normalized-korner-sandwich}
\frac1n\log_3\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})\leq\frac1n H_\kappa\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[3]}^n\right)\leq R_n^{\mathrm{lin}} 
\end{align}
where the second inequality follows from~\eqref{eq:unrestricted_vs_linear}. 
Therefore, taking the limit of both sides in~\eqref{eq:normalized-korner-sandwich},
\begin{align}
\label{eq:regularized-korner-range}
1\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n H_\kappa\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[3]}^n\right)\leq 1.5.
\end{align}
However, the lower bound in~\eqref{eq:regularized-korner-range} does not imply that the regularized K\"orner entropy equals $1$. 
The Cayley symmetry provides the exact representation of $\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\frac1n H_\kappa\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},{\bf X}_{[3]}^n\right)$ in~\eqref{eq:normalized-korner-cayley-example-2}. While the superadditivity of $\log_3 \alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})$ guarantees the existence of the limit, computing the exact value                                                                          
of $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})$ remains open. 


The asymptotic zero-error operational multiletter broadcast graph entropy~\eqref{eq:asymptotic-zero-error-broadcast-entropy} satisfies  
\begin{align}
R_0^*(3,3)                                          
\leq 1.5 \ \mbox{trits}
\end{align}
                                                                                                                                           
due to the splitting condition~\eqref{eq:normalized-korner-linear-upper}. The asymptotically lossless operational broadcast graph entropy~\eqref{eq:asymptotically-lossless-operational-entropy} satisfies
\begin{align}
1\leq R^*(3,3)                                                      
\end{align}
noting that $R^*(3,3)                                                      
\geq H_3(f_i\mid X_i)=1$ for each $i\in[3]$. 
Then, employing the hierarchy in~\eqref{eq:broadcast-entropy-hierarchy}
\begin{align}
1\leq R^*(3,3)                                                      
\leq R_0^*(3,3)                                          
\leq 1.5
\end{align}


Because $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$ is vertex-transitive under uniform distribution, its automorphisms can move the set of all maximum cliques of $G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)}$ symmetrically across all vertices. Randomly selecting an image $W$ from this set (i.e., with size exactly $\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})$), and then selecting $\mathbf X_{[3]}$ uniformly from $W$, symmetry ensures  $\mathbf X_{[3]}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb F_3^3)$. Furthermore, $\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid W=w$ is uniform over the maximum clique $w$, and thus $H_3(\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid W=w)=\log_3\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})$.  
                                                                                               
Thus, the clique entropy under the uniform source reduces to the logarithm of its clique number:
\begin{align}
R^*(3,3)\geq \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}H_\omega\left(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)},\mathbf X_{[N]}^n\right)= \limsup\limits_{n\to\infty} \frac1n \log_3\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})=1
\end{align}
where the last step follows from noting from~\eqref{eq:clique_no_limit_ex2} that  $\frac1n \log_3\omega(G_{\mathrm B}^{(n)})=1$.





Contrasting the schemes outlined above, and employing~\eqref{eq:broadcast-entropy-hierarchy} we obtain
\begin{align}
\label{eq:inequality_split_vs_one-shot_graph}
1\leq R^*(3,3)\leq R_0^*(3,3)\leq R_{\mathrm Compat.}(3,3)=1.5&<H_{\kappa,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}\left(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid\mathcal S_{[3]}\right)=1.667\nonumber\\
&<H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid \mathcal S_{[3]}=\mathbf X_{[3]})                                                                      
=1.72\nonumber\\
&<H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})=1.738 \nonumber\\
&<\operatorname{minrk}_q(\{{\bf V}_i, {\bf V}_i'\}_{i\in[3]})=R_{\mathrm{SW}}(3,3)=2 .
\end{align}
                                      
The $H_{\kappa,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}\left(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid\mathcal S_{[3]}\right)=1.667$ trits optimization                       
uses the union graph before taking blocklength products. That graph operation loses which user creates each edge. This demonstrates a strict multiletter gain and shows that the single-letter conditional independent-set expression need not tensorize, and should be interpreted as a single-letter achievable bound rather than the operational broadcast graph entropy. 

 

Both the compatibility function-based approach~\cite{ravi2016broadcast} (part ii) with rate $R_{\mathrm Compat.}(3,3)=1.5$ trits given in~\eqref{ex-linear-rate-split}, and the subspace decomposition-based approach of~\cite[Example~2]{yao2024capacity},  optimal under linear encoding, with $R_n^{\mathrm{lin}}$  in~\eqref{eq:normalized-korner-linear-upper} exploit precisely this lost information, which is not surprising because a vector linear code can exploit cross-coordinate alignment that a single-letter randomized independent-set construction cannot represent.
<!-- end ex-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{remark-Non-Linear-Vector-Encoding}
In the $(K, N, \{\mathcal{S}_i\}_{i\in[K]}, \{f_i\}_{i\in[K]})$ linear computation broadcast setting, Example~\ref{ex-linear-comp-jafar} shows that a data-splitting-based linear vector encoding scheme, building on the fixed-length minrank framework~\cite{LeeDimaindex} and exploiting source statistics yields savings in the average broadcast length compared to~\eqref{eq-theo-general-achievable-rate-via-broadcast}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- ex-3: body_md -->
\label{ex:nonlinear-outperforms-linear}

Consider the $26$-user unicast index-coding instance of~\cite{sharififar2021broadcast}, interpreted as a computation-broadcast problem. The sources $X_1,\ldots,X_{26}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathrm{Bern}}\left(\frac12\right)$ are mutually independent, user $i$ requests the linear function $f_i(\mathbf X_{[26]})=X_i$, $i\in[26]$, and has access to the side-information set $\mathcal S_i$ specified in~\cite{sharififar2021broadcast}. 



For each user, define the interfering set $\mathcal B_i\triangleq[26]\setminus\bigl(\mathcal A_i\cup\{i\}\bigr)$, where $\mathcal A_i$ indexes the sources available to user~$i$ as side information. Consider the interfering sets
\begin{align}
\label{eq:nonlinear-example-interference-sets}
\mathcal B_1&=\{2,3,5,11,12,13,15,22,23,24,25,26\},\quad
\mathcal B_2=\{1,3,6,11,12,13,16,21,22,24,25,26\},\nonumber\\
\mathcal B_3&=\{1,2,4,11,12,13,14,21,22,23,24,26\},\nonumber\\
\mathcal B_4&=\{5,6,14,15,16\},\quad
\mathcal B_5=\{4,6,14,15,16\},\quad
\mathcal B_6=\{4,5,14,15,16\},\quad
\mathcal B_7=\{17\},\nonumber\\
\mathcal B_8&=\{1,5,7,11,15,17,18\},\quad
\mathcal B_9=\{2,6,7,12,16,17,19\},\quad
\mathcal B_{10}=\{3,4,7,13,14,17,20\},\nonumber\\
\mathcal B_{11}&=\{1,2,3,5,12,13,15,21,23,24,25,26\},\quad
\mathcal B_{12}=\{1,2,3,6,11,13,16,21,22,23,25,26\},\nonumber\\
\mathcal B_{13}&=\{1,2,3,4,11,12,14,21,22,23,24,25\},\quad
\mathcal B_{14}=\{4\},\quad
\mathcal B_{15}=\{5\},\quad
\mathcal B_{16}=\{6\},\quad
\mathcal B_{17}=\{7\},\nonumber\\
\mathcal B_{18}&=\{1,5,7,8,11,15,17\},\quad
\mathcal B_{19}=\{2,6,7,9,12,16,17\},\quad
\mathcal B_{20}=\{3,4,7,10,13,14,17\},\nonumber\\
\mathcal B_{21}&=\{4,6,14,16,22\},\quad
\mathcal B_{22}=\{4,6,14,16,21\},\quad
\mathcal B_{23}=\{4,5,14,15,24\},\nonumber\\
\mathcal B_{24}&=\{4,5,14,15,23\},\quad
\mathcal B_{25}=\{5,6,15,16,26\},\quad
\mathcal B_{26}=\{5,6,15,16,25\}.
\end{align}
Thus, user~$i$ observes the side-information set
\begin{align}
\mathcal S_i=\{X_j:j\in\mathcal A_i\},
\qquad
\mathcal A_i=[26]\setminus\bigl(\mathcal B_i\cup\{i\}\bigr) , \quad i\in[26].
\end{align}
The broadcast graph for this instance is $G_{\mathrm B}\triangleq\left(\mathbb F_2^{26},\mathcal E_{\mathrm B}\right)$, where, for distinct $\mathbf x,\widetilde{\mathbf x}\in\mathbb F_2^{26}$,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:26-variable-broadcast-graph}
\{\mathbf x_{[26]},\widetilde{\mathbf x}_{[26]}\}\in\mathcal E_{\mathrm B}\iff\exists i\in[26]:\quad \mathbf x_{\mathcal A_i}=\widetilde{\mathbf x}_{\mathcal A_i}\quad\text{and}\quad f_i(\mathbf x_{[26]})=x_i\neq f_i(\widetilde{\mathbf x}_{[26]})=\widetilde x_i .
\end{align}
Because adjacency depends only on the difference $\mathbf x_{[26]}-\widetilde{\mathbf x}_{[26]}$, $G_{\mathrm B}$ is a Cayley graph and is thus vertex-transitive.



\begin{prop}[The advantage of non-linear encoding over scalar and vector linear encoding]
\label{prop:advantage_nonlinear}
The instance described in Example~\ref{ex:nonlinear-outperforms-linear} has the following properties (cf.~\cite{sharififar2021broadcast}):
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item The set $\mathcal J=\{1,2,3,11,12,13\}$
satisfies $\mathcal S_i\subseteq\mathbf X_{\mathcal J^c}$, $i\in \mathcal J$, where $\mathcal J^c=[26]\backslash \mathcal J$.
\item There exists a scalar non-linear zero-error encoder with six binary transmissions, $M=(Z_1,\ldots,Z_6)\in\mathbb F_2^6$.
\item No scalar or vector linear encoder over $\mathbb F_2$ attains rate $6$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}


\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:advantage_nonlinear}]
The sender broadcasts the following six binary symbols (see~\cite[Proposition 6]{sharififar2021broadcast}):
\begin{align}
\label{eq:nonlinear-example-encoder}
Z_1={}&X_1\oplus X_4\oplus X_6\oplus X_7
       \oplus X_{10}\oplus X_{21},\nonumber\\
Z_2={}&X_{11}\oplus X_{14}\oplus X_{16}\oplus X_{17}
       \oplus X_{20}\oplus X_{22}\oplus (X_4X_6\oplus X_4X_7\oplus X_6X_7) ,\nonumber\\
Z_3={}&X_2\oplus X_4\oplus X_5\oplus X_7
       \oplus X_8\oplus X_{23},\nonumber\\
Z_4={}&X_{12}\oplus X_{14}\oplus X_{15}\oplus X_{17}
       \oplus X_{18}\oplus X_{24}\oplus (X_4X_5\oplus X_4X_7\oplus X_5X_7) ,\nonumber\\
Z_5={}&X_3\oplus X_5\oplus X_6\oplus X_7
       \oplus X_9\oplus X_{25},\nonumber\\
Z_6={}&X_{13}\oplus X_{15}\oplus X_{16}\oplus X_{17}
       \oplus X_{19}\oplus X_{26}\oplus (X_5X_6\oplus X_5X_7\oplus X_6X_7) .
\end{align}
Because $Z_2$, $Z_4$, and $Z_6$ contain quadratic Boolean terms, the encoder $\psi:\mathbb F_2^{26}\to\mathbb{F}_2^6$ in~\eqref{eq:nonlinear-example-encoder} is nonlinear. 

Using the broadcast message $M=(Z_1,\ldots,Z_6)$ and its side information, every user can recover its requested source symbol. Hence, the non-linear scheme achieves
\begin{align}
R^{\mathrm{non-lin}}=6 \,\mbox{bits} .
\end{align}
Moreover, the set $\mathcal J=\{1,2,3,11,12,13\}$ forms a maximum acyclic induced subgraph, i.e., none of these six users knows another source indexed by $\mathcal J$, yielding the matching converse (from the MAIS lower bound~\eqref{eq:MAIS_bound})
\begin{align}
R^*(26,26)\geq 6 \,\mbox{bits} .
\end{align}
However, no scalar or vector linear encoder over $\mathbb F_2$ can attain rate $6$ for the side-information pattern in~\eqref{eq:nonlinear-example-interference-sets}, i.e., $R^{*\mathrm{lin}}>6$ bits. 
For blocklength $n$, a linear encoder has the form $M_n=\sum\limits_{j} \mathbf H_j X_j^n$ where $\mathbf H_j\in\mathbb F_2^{R\times n}$ is the matrix multiplying message $X_j^n$. After user $i$ substracts its side information, it observes $\mathbf H_iX_i^n+\sum\limits_{j\in\mathcal B_i} \mathbf H_jX_j^n$. It can recover $X_i^n$ only if the subspace generated by $\mathbf H_i$ contributes $n$ new dimensions beyond the interfering subspace:
\begin{align}
\label{eq:decodability_condition}
\operatorname{{\rm rk}}(\mathbf H_{\{i\}\cup \mathcal B_i})=\operatorname{{\rm rk}}(\mathbf H_{\mathcal B_i})+n .
\end{align}
If instead, $\operatorname{{\rm rk}}(\mathbf H_{\{i\}\cup \mathcal B_i})=\operatorname{{\rm rk}} (\mathbf H_{\mathcal B_i})$, then $\mathbf H_i$ lies in the span of the interference, and user $i$ cannot decode $X_i^n$. 
If a vector-linear code of rate $6$ existed, its entire broadcast space would have dimension $6n$ for a blocklength $n$, and every encoding matrix $\mathbf H_{j'}$ would have to be expressed as a linear combination of $\mathbf H_j$, $j\in \mathcal J$. 
Every linear combination carried by $(X_4^n,X_{14}^n)$ can already be generated using the encoding subspaces of $(X_5^n,X_{15}^n)$ and $(X_6^n,X_{16}^n)$. Because $\mathrm{col}(\mathbf H_4)\subseteq \mathrm{span}\{\mathbf H_5,\mathbf H_6,\mathbf H_{15},\mathbf H_{16}\}$, the contribution $\mathbf H_4X_4^n$ does not occupy any new dimension beyond interference, i.e., $\operatorname{{\rm rk}}(\mathbf H_{\{4\}\cup \mathcal B_4})=\operatorname{{\rm rk}} (\mathbf H_{\mathcal B_4})$. However, decodability requires $\operatorname{{\rm rk}}(\mathbf H_{\{4\}\cup \mathcal B_4})=\operatorname{{\rm rk}}(\mathbf H_{\mathcal B_4})+n$. Thus, $X_4^n$ is not decodable. Similarly, users $5$ and $6$ cannot decode their desired functions.
 
Thus, even though every demanded function $f_i({\bf X}_{[26]})=X_i$ is linear,
\begin{align}
R^*(26,26)=R^{\mathrm{non-lin}}=6<R^{*\mathrm{lin}} .
\end{align}
The strict improvement is entirely due to non-linear broadcast encoding.
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      
                                                                                                          
\end{proof}

Thus, in this example, non-linear encoding strictly outperforms scalar and vector linear encoding.


                               
\begin{prop}[Zero-error and asymptotically lossless operational broadcast entropies]
\label{prop:nonlinear-graph-separation}
For the computation-broadcast instance described above,
\begin{align}
\omega(G_{\mathrm B})=\chi(G_{\mathrm B})=64,\qquad \alpha(G_{\mathrm B})=2^{20},
\end{align}
and, under the uniform source distribution,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:clique_korner_chrom}
H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[26]})=H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[26]})=H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[26]})=6 .
\end{align}
Furthermore, the zero-error and asymptotically lossless operational broadcast entropies coincide.
\begin{align}
R_0^*(26,26)                                             
=R^*(26,26)                                                       
=6<R^{*\mathrm{lin}}.
\end{align}
\end{prop}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:nonlinear-graph-separation}]
Let $G_{\mathrm B}=(\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B}), \mathcal E(G_{\mathrm B}))$                                                   
, with $\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B})=\mathbb F_2^{26}$ and $|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B}|=2^{26}$. Two realizations are adjacent if some user has the same side information under both realizations but requests different values.

The users indexed by $\mathcal J=\{1,2,3,11,12,13\}$ form an independent set in the side-information graph, i.e., none of these users knows another source indexed by $\mathcal J$. 
 
Fixing the sources $\mathbf X_{\mathcal J^c}$ and allowing $\mathbf X_{\mathcal J}$ to vary freely produces $2^6$ source realizations. Any two distinct realizations differ in at least one coordinate $X_i$, $i\in\mathcal J$, while user $i$'s side information is unchanged. Hence, the corresponding vertices are adjacent in $G_{\mathrm B}$, and $\omega(G_{\mathrm B})\geq 2^6=64$. 
On the other hand, the non-linear construction broadcasts $M=(Z_1,\ldots,Z_6)$ (cf.~\eqref{eq:nonlinear-example-encoder}), and permits zero-error reconstruction at every user. Thus, the message induces a proper coloring $G_{\mathrm B}$ with at most $64$ colors. Hence,
\begin{align}
\label{eq:omega_lower_bound_ex3}
64\leq\omega(G_{\mathrm B})\leq\chi(G_{\mathrm B})\leq64 .
\end{align}


Because adjacency depends only on the difference between two source vectors, $G_{\mathrm B}$ is a Cayley graph and is therefore vertex-transitive. The clique--coclique bound gives
\begin{align}
\label{eq:clique_coclique_ex3}
\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})\omega(G_{\mathrm B})
\leq|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B})|=2^{26} .
\end{align}
Hence, from~\eqref{eq:omega_lower_bound_ex3} and~\eqref{eq:clique_coclique_ex3}, $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})\leq2^{20}$. 
Conversely, the $2^6$-coloring partitions $2^{26}$ vertices into $2^6$ independent color classes. At least one class must contain at least $2^{20}$ vertices, so $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})\geq 2^{20}$. Hence, $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})=2^{20}$, and 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:kappa_bound_ex3}
H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[26]})=\log_2\frac{|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B}|}{\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})}=\log_2\frac{2^{26}}{2^{20}}=6 .
\end{align}
The non-linear six-bit coloring construction in~\eqref{eq:nonlinear-example-encoder} yields                    
$\psi_1^{-1}(M)\triangleq \{\mathbf x_{[26]}\in\mathbb F_2^{26} : \phi(\mathbf x_{[26]})=M\}\in\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$ such that $|\psi_1^{-1}(M)|=2^{20}=\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})$ for every $M\in \mathbb F_2^6$. Thus, the non-linear encoder partitions $G_{\mathrm B}$ into $64$ maximum independent sets. 
Moreover, under the uniform distribution, every independent color class has the same probability, $\frac{\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})}{2^{26}}=2^{-6}$. 
Thus, the encoder in~\eqref{eq:nonlinear-example-encoder} achieves the optimal  one-shot zero-error chromatic entropy:
\begin{comment}
                                  
             
                                                         
                                                                                                                  
           
                                                                                               
                                                                                                         
\end{comment}
\begin{align}
H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[26]})=6 .
\end{align}
 
Finally, vertex transitivity and $\omega(G_{\mathrm B})=2^6$ give
\begin{align}
H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[26]})=\log_2\omega(G_{\mathrm B})=6 .
\end{align}


                               
Repeating the non-linear code componentwise gives a zero-error rate of $6$ at every blocklength. Conversely, after revealing                                  
$\mathbf X_{\mathcal J^c}$, the message must enable the recovery of                      
sources indexed by 
$\mathcal J$. Thus, Fano’s inequality gives $H_2\left(\mathbf X_{\mathcal J}^n\middle|,M_n,\mathbf X_{\mathcal J^c}^n\right)=6n-o(n)$. 
After normalization and passage to the limit, this proves $R^*(26,26)                                                       
\geq6$.


From the impossibility result~\cite[Theorem 3]{sharififar2021broadcast} for scalar and vector linear coding in the baseline $26$-user instance  
\begin{equation}
R_0^*(26,26)                                             
=R^*(26,26)                                                       
=6<R^{*\mathrm{lin}}.\qedhere
\end{equation}
\end{proof}
<!-- end ex-3 -->

       \oplus X_9\oplus X_{25},\nonumber\\

       \oplus X_{19}\oplus X_{26}\oplus (X_5X_6\oplus X_5X_7\oplus X_6X_7) .

Every linear combination carried by $(X_4^n,X_{14}^n)$ can already be generated using the encoding subspaces of $(X_5^n,X_{15}^n)$ and $(X_6^n,X_{16}^n)$. Thus, even though every demanded function $f_i({\bf X}_{[26]})=X_i$ is linear,
\begin{align}
R^*(26,26)=R^{\mathrm{non-lin}}=6<R^{*\mathrm{lin}} .
\end{align}
The strict improvement is entirely due to non-linear broadcast encoding.

, with $\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B})=\mathbb F_2^{26}$ and $|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B}|=2^{26}$. Two realizations are adjacent if some user has the same side information under both realizations but requests different values.

The users indexed by $\mathcal J=\{1,2,3,11,12,13\}$ form an independent set in the side-information graph, i.e., none of these users knows another source indexed by $\mathcal J$. 
 
Fixing the sources $\mathbf X_{\mathcal J^c}$ and allowing $\mathbf X_{\mathcal J}$ to vary freely produces $2^6$ source realizations. Any two distinct realizations differ in at least one coordinate $X_i$, $i\in\mathcal J$, while user $i$'s side information is unchanged. Hence, the corresponding vertices are adjacent in $G_{\mathrm B}$, and $\omega(G_{\mathrm B})\geq 2^6=64$. 
On the other hand, the non-linear construction broadcasts $M=(Z_1,\ldots,Z_6)$ (cf.~\eqref{eq:nonlinear-example-encoder}), and permits zero-error reconstruction at every user. Thus, the message induces a proper coloring $G_{\mathrm B}$ with at most $64$ colors. Hence,

Conversely, the $2^6$-coloring partitions $2^{26}$ vertices into $2^6$ independent color classes. At least one class must contain at least $2^{20}$ vertices, so $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})\geq 2^{20}$. Hence, $\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})=2^{20}$, and 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:kappa_bound_ex3}
H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[26]})=\log_2\frac{|\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B}|}{\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})}=\log_2\frac{2^{26}}{2^{20}}=6 .
\end{align}
The non-linear six-bit coloring construction in~\eqref{eq:nonlinear-example-encoder} yields                    
$\psi_1^{-1}(M)\triangleq \{\mathbf x_{[26]}\in\mathbb F_2^{26} : \phi(\mathbf x_{[26]})=M\}\in\Gamma(G_{\mathrm B})$ such that $|\psi_1^{-1}(M)|=2^{20}=\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})$ for every $M\in \mathbb F_2^6$. \end{comment}
\begin{align}
H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[26]})=6 .
\end{align}
 
Finally, vertex transitivity and $\omega(G_{\mathrm B})=2^6$ give
\begin{align}
H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[26]})=\log_2\omega(G_{\mathrm B})=6 .
\end{align}


                               
Repeating the non-linear code componentwise gives a zero-error rate of $6$ at every blocklength. Conversely, after revealing                                  
$\mathbf X_{\mathcal J^c}$, the message must enable the recovery of                      
sources indexed by 
$\mathcal J$. Thus, Fano’s inequality gives $H_2\left(\mathbf X_{\mathcal J}^n\middle|,M_n,\mathbf X_{\mathcal J^c}^n\right)=6n-o(n)$. 
After normalization and passage to the limit, this proves $R^*(26,26)                                                       
\geq6$.

<!-- ex-4: body_md -->
\label{ex-nonlinear-comp_self-complementary_graph}

Consider a NFCB model $(3,3,\{X_i\}_{i\in[3]},\{f_i\}_{i\in[3]})$, where ${\bf X}_{[3]}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb{F}_2^3)$, and the desired functions are 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:function_demands_self_complementary}
f_1=(X_2\oplus X_3,X_2(X_2\oplus X_3)) , \quad
f_2=X_1\oplus X_3, \quad
f_3=X_1\oplus X_2 . 
\end{align}
These functions yield the respective set of edges in the corresponding $G_{f_i}$ for each user $i\in[3]$:
\begin{align}
\mathcal E_1=\{&\{000,001\},\{000,010\},\{001,010\},\{001,011\},\{010,011\},\nonumber\\
&\{100,101\},\{100,110\},\{101,110\},
\{101,111\},\{110,111\}\} , \\
 
\mathcal E_2=\{&\{000,001\},\{000,100\},\{001,101\},\{100,101\},\{010,011\},\{010,110\},\{011,111\},\{110,111\}\} , \\
 
\mathcal E_3=\{&\{000,010\},\{000,100\},\{010,110\},\{100,110\},\{001,011\},\{001,101\},\{011,111\},\{101,111\}\} .
\end{align}
Thus, taking their union gives a fourteen-edge broadcast graph $G_{\mathrm B}=(\mathcal{V}(G_{\mathrm B}),\mathcal{E}(G_{\mathrm B}))$, with $\mathcal{V}(G_{\mathrm B})=\mathbb F_2^3$ and $\mathcal{E}(G_{\mathrm B})=\bigcup_{i=1}^3 \mathcal{E}_i$, 
and is shown in Figure~\ref{fig:Graphs_ex4}. 
 
The following vertex permutation $\pi$ maps $G_{\mathrm B}$ to $\overline{G}_{\mathrm B}$: 
\begin{align}
\begin{array}{c|cccccccc}
\mathbf X_{[3]}
&000&001&010&011&100&101&110&111\\ \hline
\pi(\mathbf X_{[3]})
&001&100&111&010&110&011&000&101 .
\end{array}
\end{align}
Hence, $G_{\mathrm B}\simeq\overline{G}_{\mathrm B}$, implying $\{u,v\}\in\mathcal E(G_{\mathrm B})\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\, \{\pi(u),\pi(v)\}\in\mathcal E(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B})$. 
 
Both $G_{\mathrm B}$ and $\overline{G}_{\mathrm B}$ have $|\mathcal{V}(G_{\mathrm B})|=8$ vertices and $|\mathcal{E}(G_{\mathrm B})|=14$ edges, and the same degree sequence. Furthermore, 
\begin{align}
\label{chromatic_numbers_self_complementary}
\alpha(G_{\mathrm B})=\omega(G_{\mathrm B})=\chi(G_{\mathrm B})=3=\omega(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B})=\chi(\overline{G}_{\mathrm B})=3 \ .
\end{align}


                                      
\begin{figure*}[t!]
    \centering
\includegraphics[width=0.5\textwidth]{plots/Ex3-graphs.png}
    \caption{\small (a) A self-complementary broadcast graph $G_{\mathrm B}$ on $8$ vertices and (b) its complement $\overline{G}_{\mathrm B}$.}
    \label{fig:Graphs_ex4}
\end{figure*}



The graph consists of four triangles $\{000,001,010\}$, $\{001,010,011\}$, $\{100,101,110\}$, and $\{101,110,111\}$, which form two overlapping triangle pairs, where the left block is composed of $\{100,101,110\}$, $\{101,110,111\}$, and the right block consists of $\{000,001,010\}$, $\{001,010,011\}$, where each pair shares an edge. 
 
There are $7$ different $4$-vertex sets that contain a $4$-cycle, of which $5$ are chordless (induced $C_4$) and $2$ are chorded. Specifically, chordless $4$-cycles are $\{000,001,100,101\}$, $\{000,010,100,110\}$, $\{001,010,101,110\}$, $\{001,011,101,111\}$, and $\{010,011,110,111\}$, and chorded $4$-cycles are $\{000,001,010,011\}$, and $\{100,101,110,111\}$. 
 
Furthermore, $G_{\mathrm B}$ contains neither an induced $C_5$ nor an induced $C_7$. Since $\mathcal V(G_{\mathrm B})=8$, these are the only possible induced odd cycles of length at least five. By self-complementarity, $\overline{G}_{\mathrm B}$ also contains neither an induced $C_5$ nor an induced $C_7$.                                                                           
By the Strong Perfect Graph Theorem, $G_{\mathrm B}$ is perfect.                                                                                                                                                          
 
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   
Hence, from~\cite[Lemma~12]{alon1996source} and~\cite{csiszar1990entropy}, 
\begin{align}
\label{eq:clique_entropy_self-complementary_perfect_ex3}
H_\omega(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})=H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})=1.5 \,\mbox{bits} .
\end{align}
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             

   
Employing the Slepian-Wolf theorem~\cite{SlepWolf1973}, $R_{\mathrm{SW}}(3,3)=\max\limits_{i\in[3]}\,  H_2({\bf X}_{[3]\backslash i})=2$ bits.


   
The optimal one-shot zero-error rate is $R_{1,0}^*(3,3)=\log_2 \chi(G_{\mathrm B})=\log_2 3=1.585$ bits.
To determine the entropy of one-shot coloring, let $C=c_{G_{\mathrm B}}(\mathbf X_{[3]})\in\{R,B,G\}$ denote the color assigned to the source realization, where
\begin{align}
c_{G_{\mathrm B}}^{-1}(R)=\{000,011,101\},\quad
c_{G_{\mathrm B}}^{-1}(B)=\{010,100,111\},\quad
c_{G_{\mathrm B}}^{-1}(G)=\{001,110\} .
\end{align}
Under $\mathbf X_{[3]}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathbb F_2^3)$, its distribution is $\bigl(\mathbb{P}(C=R),\mathbb{P}(C=B),\mathbb{P}(C=G)\bigr)=\left(0.375,0.375,0.25\right)$. Thus,
\begin{align}
H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})=\mathsf H_2((0.375,0.375,0.25))=1.561 \ \mbox{bits} .
\end{align}

For $X_1$, conditioning on $X_1=0$ leaves the four vertices $\{000,001,010,011\}$. Their induced graph is $K_4$ with one edge removed. The same structure appears when $X_1=1$. A minimum-entropy coloring of each induced graph has color-class sizes $2,1,1$. Because the four vertices are conditionally uniform, the color probabilities are $(0.5,0.25,0.25)$, leading to $H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]} \mid X_1)=1.5$ bits. 
For $X_j$, $j=2,3$, conditioning on $X_j=0$ or $X_j=1$ produces an induced $C_4$. Thus, $H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]} \mid X_j)=1$ bit. Hence,~$H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid \mathcal S_{[3]}=\mathbf X_{[3]})                                                                      
=1.5$ bits.


Given the functions in~\eqref{eq:function_demands_self_complementary}, for $f_1$, we have $\mathbb{P}(f_1=(0,0))=0.5$, and $\mathbb{P}(f_1=(1,0))=\mathbb{P}(f_1=(1,1))=0.25$. Thus, $H_2(f_1)=H_2(f_1\mid X_1)=\mathsf H_2((0.5,0.25,0.25))=1.5$ bits because $f_1$ is independent of $X_1$. 
For any zero-error blocklength-$n$ code, user one must recover $f_1^n$ from $(M_n,X_1^n)$. Thus, $H_2(f_1^n\mid M_n,X_1^n)=0$. Therefore,
\begin{align}
H_2(M_n)\geq H_2(M_n\mid X_1^n)\geq I_2(f_1^n;M_n\mid X_1^n)=H_2(f_1^n\mid X_1^n)=1.5n \ .
\end{align}
Thus, $R_0^*(3,3)                                            
\geq 1.5$ bits. In other words, the K\"orner-entropy upper bound and the single-user converse coincide.

For asymptotically lossless reconstruction, employing Fano's inequality
\begin{align}
H_2(f_1^n\mid M_n,X_1^n)=o(n) ,
\end{align}
and therefore, $H_2(M_n)\geq 1.5n-o(n)$, yielding $R^*(3,3)                                                      
\geq 1.5$ bits.


Thus, for the given perfect graph setting,  the operational quantities are optimal:
\begin{multline}
\label{eq:rate_comparison_ex3}
R^*(3,3)=R_0^*(3,3)                                                                                                   
= H_\kappa(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]})=H_{\chi,\mathrm B}^{\mathrm{SI}}(G_{\mathrm B},\mathbf X_{[3]}\mid \mathcal S_{[3]}=\mathbf X_{[3]})                                                                      
=1.5\\
\leq  H_\chi(G_{\mathrm B},{\bf X}_{[3]})=1.561 \leq R_{\mathrm{SW}}(3,3)=2 \ .
\end{multline}

                          

In this example, where $G_{\mathrm B}$ is self-complementary and perfect, our proposed technique achieves the optimum.
